rOKEWOED 

Within the last decade the motion picture has de- 
veloped to such an extent that it has become a tremen- 
dous factor and influence in our national consciousness. 
It is no longer merely the " second largest industry in 
the world." It is a new art form, the youngest of all 
the arts perhaps, but distinct and separate from them. 
Like all new things the photoplay has countless chan- 
nels unexplored, and many possibilities undiscovered. 
It has yet to be tried by the tests of thought and time. 
It is impossible, therefore, in this early stage of develop- 
ment to lay down iron-clad rules to govern the new, art. 
Its growth and progress demand constant expansion and 
experimentation. 

Yet it is because of my supreme faith in the future 
of the photoplay and my urgent desire to do whatever 
little I can toward awakening people to the realization 
of its latent possibilities that I have endeavored to set 
down in the following pages some of the thoughts that 
have come to me in lecturing upon the motion picture 
at Columbia University. The work there was initiated 
with the dual purpose of bringing the public at large to 
a more real appreciation of the best in photoplays and 
by that appreciation to demand the best that can be at- 
tained by the producers, and secondly to oflfer prospec- 
tive writers of photoplays a short cut upon the road to 
ultimate success. This book has been written with the 



vi FOREWORD 

same aims in mind. Some of the ideas contained herein 
have already been expressed in lectures to the students 
of Columbia University from the summer of 1917 imtil 
the present time, in the Photoplay Magazine, and in 
lectures to The Women's Club and the National Board 
of Review of Motion Pictures. 

I should like to express my gratitude to the Famous 
Players-Lasky Company for the privilege of reprinting 
the continuity of the Columbia Prize photoplay " Witch- 
craft," and to Mr. Robert MacAlamey of that Company 
for his unfailing courtesy and kindly spirit of co-opera- 
tion, which has been of inestimable value, also to Dr. 
Victor Oscar Freeburg, whose interesting lectures and 
writings laid the cornerstone for my work. 

F. T. P. 
New York, June 1, 1920.

CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 



CHAPTER I 

THE ART AND THE SCIENCE 

The study of the photoplay may be approached from 
either of two angles. The first is from the cultural stand- 
point. A person may desire to learn as much as possible 
about the photoplay as an art form, its latent powers, 
its claim to sestheticism. He may take up the study of 
scenario technique in order to appreciate the more the 
efforts of producers, or to criticize them intelligently. 
He may argue that if the photoplay is worthy of the ex- 
penditure of three or four evenings a week upon it, as 
a matter of general information, it ought to be worthy 
of careful study and consideration. Or it may be that 
people feel that they ought to be able to form new and 
bright opinions u|)on the latest photoplay just as well as 
upon the latest books, or the latest music, or the latest 
plays, or the latest turn in the political situation. Not 
every student of art aims to surpass Leonardo de Vinci 
or Michelangelo, nor does every student of versification 
hope to dim the glory of Homer or Virgil. Not every 
student of military strategy plans to out-manoeuver Alex- 
ander or Csesar or Napoleon, nor every student of the 
drama expect to steal the laurels of Sophocles, Shake- 



2 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

speare, or Moliere. Neither does every student of the 
photoplay plan to produce plays. Very often their only 
object in studying the art is to become conversant with 
the working principles of an art which has become one 
of the most popular forms of diversion. 

There are, however, a great many students who ap- 
proach the photoplay with the idea of ultimate produc- 
tion in mind. And this gives us our second point of 
view — ^the practical aspect of photoplay composition. 
Writing for the screen is an art, but it is also a science. 
The writer must be at once an artist and an artisan. 
Theoretical knowledge is essential and forms a strong 
foundation for later building. It must be supplemented, 
however, by practical experience. The student must 
follow the pedagogical principle — " learn by doing." In 
the case of the photoplay he must not only write 
scenarios, but he must have them produced. A photo- 
play has no objective reality until it reaches the screen. 
Closet drama may exist, if you will — the plays of Tenny- 
son and Swinburne may be read with the deepest enjoy- 
ment in the library — but there can be no closet photo- 
drama. A stage play in manuscript form may be 
literature, although not necessarily so. The dialogue 
exists exactly as it is to be upon the stage with only the 
voices and mannerisms of the actors and actresses lack- 
ing. The action is usually written in. The plays of 
Shaw, Galsworthy, Barrie, Pinero, Jones, are almost as 
popular as reading matter in the libraries as are novels 
and short stories of a like literary flavor. Not so with 
the photoplay. A photoplay does not actually become 
one until it exists in film. Its appeal is through the 



THE ART AND THE SCIENCE S 

medium of the picture. And although the pictures may 
be suggested in the manuscript, they do not exist as such 
until the production is made. The author of the manu- 
script furnishes the idea which goes through the hands 
of the scenario editor, the director, and the actors before 
it appears in picture form. 

There are certain fundamental principles which every 
prospective writer of motion pictures should be familiar 
with. He must know the stages of development through 
which the photoplay has passed. He must know the 
technique of the photoplay, which is individual in the 
last degree. The photoplay has a language of its own — 
the language of the camera. If the student is to become 
a successful scenarioist he must understand the limita- 
tions and powers of the camera in order that he may 
. treat his subject matter accordingly. This book en- 
deavors to give the student training in the fundamentals 
of the art of picture making. It attempts to furnish 
him with the necessary equipment — ^the tools of his pro- 
fession. He must never lose sight, however, of the fact 
that his ultimate success depends upon his individual 
effort. Training and academic counsel may aid in re- 
casting and in making the most of his ideas. It may 
even stimulate and awaken thought. But the ideas them- 
selves he, and he alone, must furnish. Likewise he must 
furnish the perseverance and the capacity for hard work 
and sustained effort which is so essential to accomplish- 
ment 

In the hands of a trained writer the power of the 
photoplay is illimitable. There is an ever increasing 
demand for skilled writers, of original stories, of con- 



4 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

tinuities, and of cinematic criticisms, but in order to 
fill this demand the writer must be trained until his 
abilily is indisputabla Only the sweeping extensiveness 
of the industry itself can be a fitting gauge of the colossal 
need for photoplays and photoplay writers. But photo- 
plays cannot be built in a day, nor photoplay writers in 
a week nor a month. Perfection is a hard taskmaster. 
Even the experienced man of letters must cast over- 
board the equipment that he has been years perhaps in 
acquiring and learn the new art of motion pictures, 
which is as different from fiction writing as painting is 
different from music. After all, art is long and time 
is fleeting, and the path of the cinema composer is a 
far and thorny one. Yet the pot of gold which lies at 
the end of the rainbow which is sometimes called success, 
ought to be enough to keep hope alive in the hearts of 
the photoplaywrights. They must prepare themselves 
now against the day when their services will be at the 
highest premium. 



CHAPTER II 
THE PLOT 

Although the photoplay is a new art it has much in 
common with the older arts^ and borrows from them^ if 
you will, certain laws, principles, and appeals. From 
pantomime it borrows the art of expressing thought 
through motion; from painting it borrows the art of 
pictorial composition; from sculpture it takes the grace- 
ful lines and groupings of human figures ; and from the ^ 
drama it borrows the element of plot and the revelation 
of character through action. 

Plot is a careful and logical working out of the laws " 
of cause and effect. The mere sequence of events will 
not make a plot. Emphasis must be laid upon causality 
and the action and reaction of the human will. In order 
to have plot we must start a struggle, present a problem, 
or suggest a complication. The audience will then be 
in a state of suspense as to the outcome of the struggle, 
the solution of the problem or the resolution of the 
complication, and we shall have a plot. All authorities 
on the drama are agreed upon this point regarding the 
necessity of struggle. Goethe in "Wilhelm Meister" 
says he believes conflict is necessary to all drama. Aris- 
totle says that struggle is one of the essentials. Archer 
claims that climax is the important factor rather than 

conflicting forces, but after all, as Professor Brander 

6 



6 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

Matthews points out, what is climax but the clash of 
forces ? Henry Arthur Jones puts it in the vernacular : 
^^ Drama exists when a man is up against it." Very 
true, but when a man is " up against it " he struggles. 
Therefore we come back to the inevitable struggle. Prof. 
Matthews calls attention further to the great interest in 
the horse races of Byzantium, the gladiatorial, conquests 
of Eome, the bull fights of Spain, the " world series " 
of to-day — their interest, one and all, is conflict Steven- 
son declares that drama exists " when duty and inclina- 
tion come nobly to the grapple '^ . . . and once niore 
we have the element of struggle. 

But although struggle must be present, plot cannot 
be based upon struggle alone, for then it would be 
heroic merely. We must have suffering, but again not 
alone, for then it would be pathetic merely. We must 
have causality, but not alone, for that would mean the 
rational element merely. All three must lend their 
elemental force to the plot. Through such a combination 
we can arouse suspense in the mind of the audience, and 
the presence of suspense determines plot. 

Suspense has been defined as a psychological trick 
for keeping people still. It constitutes the difference 
between the masterpieces in the Metropolitan Museum 
of Art and the pictures at the Eivoli or the Eialto 
Theatres. It explains why the people straggle into the 
Metropolitan by twos and threes and struggle into the 
Kialto and Eivoli by hundreds. The twos and threes • 
look at a picture in the Art Museum for less than ten 
minutes — ^unlesff they chance to be art students, critics, 
or connoisseurs. The hundreds in the Eivoli or the 



THE PLOT 7 

Bialto look at the picture for more than an hour. As far 
as beauty is concerned there can be no comparison of the 
merits of the two pictures. Yet the motion picture draws 
more people and holds them at attention longer than 
do the masterpieces, not through any intrinsic merit of 
its own, but because it depicts unfolding events, the 
outcome of which the audience is breathlessly awaiting. 
It possesses the element of struggle, which never fails 
to arouse suspense. 

The conflict which must exist in a photoplay need 
not necessarily be a physical struggle. It may be a 
mental struggle. It may be the struggle of a character 
against environment, or force of circumstances, or 
heredity, or habit, or against his own evil inclinations. 
In all of the latter cases the antagonist would not be a 
person, but an abstraction, an influence, or a moral 
force. Sometimes a character may embody or represent 
the idea or the influence, which is the real antagonist. 
In Ibsen's " A Doll's House," for instance, it is not the 
struggle between Nora and Towald that absorbs us 
it is Torvald's attitude toward his wife and her reaction 
to it. In Hugo's " Les Miserables " it is Jean Valjean's 
struggle with his past, which rises up against him at 
every turn, in spite of the noble deeds and spiritual 
heights his present has attained. This past is more or 
less incorporated into the figure of Javert. In D. W. 
Griffith's "Broken Blossoms," "Battling" Burrows, 
the pugilist, is not so much an individual as he is a type. 
He stands for cruelty. The conflict of the play is 
between the gentleness of childhood and the cruelty of 
mature physical strength, that has been indulged beyond 



8 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

all bounds. In " The Jest " the struggle is between 
brains and brawn, which attributes were admirably en- 
acted by the two Barrymores, John as Gianneto, Lionel 
as Neri. 

But whatever the nature of the struggle may be, if 
it has the power to awaken suspense in the minds of the 
spectator, its function is fulfilled. Suspense, however, 
is not the only prerequisite of plot. In addition, plot 
must be tested by the laws of logic, for just as truly as a 
builder of houses must take into account the physical 
laws of gravity, equilibrium and stress, so the builder 
of plays must follow the laws of the human mind, the 
laws of inductive and deductive reasoning, which we 
mortals were bom with, acquired, or had thrust upon us 
by the processes of education, or the taskmaster experi- 
ence. 

The old French idea of plot was this: In the first 
act, get a man up a tree. In the second act, throw 
stones at him. In the third act, get him down. This 
states in a rather unique way the structural divisions 
of a plot, Aristotle calls these divisions, the beginning, 
the middle, and the end. Scenarioists usually term them 
the premise, the complication, and the solution. The 
premise of the play is the introduction, and to it is 
assigned the work of exposition. Its function is to put 
the audience in possession of the facts that it is necessary 
for them to know in order to follow the plot with the 
least amount of effort. It should set out countless 
finger-posts which foreshadow the events that are to take 
place in the complication, yet do it so ingeniously that 
the suspense in the development of the plot will not 



THE PLOT 9 

be cut short ... as Mr. Archer formulateB the law in 
" Playmaking," "foreshadow, but do not forestall." 
The skill of the photoplaywright depends upon the way 
he manipulates his material in the premise. He must 
economize the attention of his audience as much as 
possible by making the premise clear. No constant 
allusions to things that have taken place ought to be 
necessary once the premise has gone by. There is 
nothing more annoying than a book which obliges its 
reader to turn back every few pages to find out who a 
certain character is, or what he has done, with the 
exclamation, " I don't remember him," or " where does 
she come in ? ", or, " when did that happen ? " It is 
even more exasperating in a photoplay because the audi- 
ence is unable to turn back any figurative pages. What 
has passed has passed. If we have missed part of the 
story, we must either rack our brains to think of it, or 
wait for the second showing of the picture. Often this 
is necessary because of a faulty, or inadequate exposition 
of initial facts. " Othello " is one of the best examples 
of good exposition in Shakespeare. Its first act is 
wonderfully handled. Ibsen's " Eosmersholm " is poor 
because it withholds knowledge of Eebecca West too 
long. The laws of logic demand that the premise shall 
pave the way for the complication, or that the complica- 
tion be a direct outcome of the premise, just as the 
solution is a direct outcome of the complication. The 
premise should be promising. It should be interesting 
enough to capture the attention of the spectator immedi- 
ately. There is never a great deal of emotion or suspense 
in the premise of the play. Persons who enter a motion 



10 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

picture theatre are in a state of complete neutrality 
toward the characters in the play. They know nothing 
about them and they care less. The cinema author must 
break down this neutrality gradually, and interest the 
spectators step by step in the drama that is enfolding 
before them. For this reason the premise of the play 
is never intensely dramatic. Any great tension at this 
time would be anticipating the climax. There was a 
photoplay called " The Source," in which Wallace Eeid 
starred, that opened with rather a dramatic situation. 
Before we are introduced to the characters, we are 
shown a darkened interior in which vague figures move 
about obscurely. By the dim light of a lantern, we see 
that human bodies are being carried and thrown upon 
the straw of some cavernous interior. The man with 
the lantern is a burly figure, almost hidden in an im- 
mense fur coat and hat. There is something sinister, 
mysterious, about the proceedings. We are put in a 
state of suspense immediately as to who these people 
are. Are they corpses? And if so, how have they 
met their deaths ? Is it a revolution ? Or a conspiracy ? 
Or murder? Who is responsible? Presently we find 
that the figure is a lumberman from the far North who 
has been buying intoxicated derelicts from a saloon- 
keeper at ten dollars a head and shipping them in an 
old cattle car to his lumber camp where he makes them 
prisoners and compels them to labor in his timberl^nds. 
This is a very dramatic scene and under ordinary 
circumstances might start a picture at too high a tension. 
But in this instance the story is intensely dramatic 
throughout. There are moments of suspense to follow 



THE PLOT 11 

which far exceed this initial crisis. Therefore the 
scene serves to strike the keynote of the picture, yet in 
no way robs the climax of its effect. 

In terms of reels the premise usually takes about one 
reel of continuity. The second, third and fourth reels 
are devoted to the complication, and the fifth is given 
over to the solution. 

The complication is the body of the photoplay. It 
is the logical effect of the given causes. When there are 
insufficient causes, or no causes at all, bad melodrama 
results. In a photoplay released some time ago, the 
hero had sought refuge in the loft of a barn, the loft 
being some five feet above the barn floor. The farmer 
and his hired man enter the barn in the course of the 
evening, and stand below the loft. Whereupon the hero 
leaps upon them from the loft, and both men fall dead. 
The audience is not at all prepared for this. One man 
who is jumped upon from a five foot loft might be 
knocked down, and even stunned. He would hardly be 
killed. But in this case not only one man but two had 
been killed by the impact from the leap of a single man 
who was not even the giant Maciste. 

In another play, a wife who was pictured as the 
spoiled darling of a husband who was enormously 
wealthy, with a flourishing business, had spent funds 
that belonged to the Bed Cross Society, of which she 
was Treasurer, in playing bridge for money. The 
society asks her to send a draft for the money to Head- 
quarters, whereupon she seeks out an unscrupulous man 
whom she has reason to believe admires her, and enters 
into a dishonorable arrangement with him in order to 



12 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

procure the ten thousand dollars. Here the effect is 
entirely out of proportion to the cause. If her husband 
was wealthy as the premise showed him to be^ the most 
natural thing in the world would be for the wife to ask 
him for the mioney. Otherwise he should be shown on 
the verge of financial ruin in the premisa Or else he 
should have been shown to be of very upright principles, 
insisting that she give up playing bridge for money. 
In this event she might be afraid of his wratE and go to 
someone else rather than incur it. As the play stands 
the husband is not of the awe-inspiring variety, but pets 
and spoils his wife, denying her nothing. In addition, 
the play could have been strengthened by making the 
sinister character of the villian an unknown quantity to 
the wife^ She might go to him thinking he would lend 
her the money in friendship, and discover, too late, his 
real nature. As it stands, no honorable woman, even 
under the direst stress, would seek out a man she knows 
but slightly, but whose reputation is a matter of common 
gossip, and beg for money from him. 

There may be two parts to the complication, a major 
and a minor complication, but the connection between 
the two must be organic. They must be so interwoven 
that the photoplay shows no break in the dramatic unity. 
The minor complication must be part of and dependent 
upon the major ; the two should be integral parts of the 
whole. If either can be removed from the story without 
weakening the entire strand of the complication thereby, 
the connection is not organic. In George Eliot's novel, 
" Silas Marner," we find a dual complication, two 
distinct stories skillfully blended together. There is 



THE PLOT 13 

the Nancy and Godfrey Cass story, and there is the 
story of Eppie and Silas. But Eppie is the daughter of 
Godfrey, and it was Godfrey's brother Dunstan who 
stole Silas Mamer's gold, for the loss of which Eppie 
makes ample amendment with her glittering golden 
curls. Therefore, each is a part of the other. We need 
both to make up the full complement of plot. 

In the photoplay the major and minor complication 
are often found because there is a law, almost as strong 
as the laws of the Medes and Persians, that every motion 
picture must contain a love interest Now there are a 
great many dramatic themes which make excellent 
material, but which have no love interest. So the 
photoplaywright, in order to fulfill the commercial law, 
often sets about to insert a bit of love into his story. 
In the " success " stories, the type of story that appears 
so frequently in the Saturday Evening Post, or the 
stories that Charles Eay plays in — the ne'er do well, who 
makes a man of himself — ^the love interest is subsidiary 
to the success, either financial or moral, which is por- 
trayed. Examples of these are " The Nine O^cIock 
Town," " Greased Lightning," " The Sheriff's Son," all 
of which were interpreted for the screen by Charles 
Eay, and " The Roaring Road," " The Source " and 
"Less Than Kin," in which Wallace Reid played. 

The pictures in the complication should be intensely 
dramatic to coincide with the high tension of the events 
immediately preceding the climax. The pictorial back- 
ground for the mental or moral struggle of the characters 
should be in keeping with that struggle. Used skill- 
fully, the settings should stress and intensify the action. 



14 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

But we shall consider the setting at greater length in a 
later chapter. 

The climax is that point in the play where the rising 
forces of the play culminate. It is the highest tension 
of which the plot is capable. There may be several 
crises in the play before the climax is reached. The 
photoplaywright must distinguish between crisis and 
climax. A crisis occurs when two conflicting forces 
meet and demand immediate adjustment. In the story 
of Joseph and his brethren, for instance, the favoritism 
shown toward Joseph by Jacob and his brothers' hatred 
of him bring about a conflict which comes to a head' 
with the decision of the brothers to kill Joseph. This 
is the first dramatic crisis in the story. The second 
comes when Potiphar's wife makes love to Joseph, and he 
repulses her. The third occurs when Joseph, now ruler 
of Egypt, arrests the brothers, and the silver cup, which 
he himself has caused to be placed in Benjamin's sack, 
is found. This is the highest tension of which the plot 
is capable. Joseph knows that the young Benjamin is 
dear to his father's heart. If the brothers allow him to 
be killed, Joseph will know they are still as cruel at 
heart as they were years ago, when they were willing 
to sacrifice him. Eeuben pleads to die in Benjamin's 
place, and the crisis is safely passed. Joseph knows his 
brothers have had a change of heart and forgives them. 

We spoke a little while ago of awakening suspense in 
the mind of the audience. There is something still more 
important in building a plot. It is the sustaining of 
suspense. Suspense takes the form of a question, the 
answer to which is always out of reach. It is the work 



THE PLOT 16 

of the photoplaywright to arrange his material so that 
the answer to this question be not given too soon. The 
audience must be kept constantly interested. One method 
of doing this is to balance the struck. Neither virtue 
nor villainy should have things all its own way. The 
writer must allow one to triumph for a while^ and then 
have a downfall while the other is in power, maintaining 
a dramatic see-saw between the two. Virtue must not be 
too white, nor villiany too black, lest the spectator guess 
the outcome of the struggle. 

There may be two types of suspense in the complica- 
tion of the play, forward suspense and restrospective 
suspense. In the former, interest is awakened in what 
is about to happen. The audience is constantly looking 
forward to coming events. In the latter, the audience 
is interested in what has already happened. They are 
straining into the past to see what secrets it holds. In 
this type of play the premises, or causes of the play, 
have sometimes taken place before the play begins. It 
is the effects of these causes which the action enfolds. 
In the screen play " The Firefly of France '' the entire 
story is told in retrospect. This was retrospective action 
used in. a good cause, for the incidents of the play were 
almost too stirring, the suspense almost too great, for 
the audience to bear. Having seen the hero at the 
beginning of the play hale and hearty, the audience felt 
measurably reassured, and could follow the play without 
becoming too harrowed by their ovni emotions. Practi- 
cally the use of retrospective suspense is entirely per- 
missible, provided it heightens the emotions of the 
spectators or keeps them in ignorance of some detail of 



16 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

past history "which will radically affect some character 
in the plot. Ideally, however, retrospective suspense is 
out of place in the cinema. The tense of the photoplay is 
the present tense, and therefore the audience takes it for 
granted that what is happening on the screen is happen- 
ing in chronological order. To go back into the past 
demands a break in the sequence of events and very often 
in the dramatic unity of the play, for all too often the 
things we go back to relate are, as Kipling would say, 
another story. If the author must tell a story which 
covers a long period of years and narrate the entire life 
history of his characters, let him choose the epic poem 
and not the photoplay as his art medium. On the whole, 
it is better policy to adhere to straightforward action. 

Another method of holding the audience in suspense 
is temporary or time suspensa This occurs when the 
audience knows what is going to happen, but they do 
not know exactly when it will take place. This is merely 
the awakening of a momentary interest. It is simply 
a postponement of the solution of the difficulties in which 
the characters find themselves. These momentary thrills 
which do not aflFect the main structure of the play, but 
merely heighten the interest for the moment, are not to 
be encouraged. It is bad art to put the audience in a 
state of suspense unless it sustains and reinforces the 
main dramatic interest of the play. The automobile 
which breaks down when the heroine is on the way to the 
scaffold with a pardon ; the gates of a railroad crossing, 
which close just as the pursuers are upon the villain ; the 
man who finds his cartridge chamber empty just as he 
is about to fire upon his enemy ; these, and a hundred 



.^ 



THE PLOT 17 

other devices for prolonging the agony of the audience, 
can be thought up with little effort and no art, and in- 
corporated into a photoplay. In a screen play lately 
released nearly all these momentary thrills might have 
answered " present." A man who believes he is dying 
of heart failure takes upon himself the crime of the 
brother of the girl he loves. The girl discovers that her 
brother never committed the crime, but that it was the 
deed of an insane uncle, who has told the hero he has 
heart failure purely out of sardonic glee. The girl 
reaches the Governor with her evidence on the very day 
of the execution. The Governor, however, is out to 
lunch. When she finally obtains an audience she con- 
vinces him of the man^s innocence. The Governor is 
afraid it is too late. The execution is scheduled for 
6 :45. They look at the clock. It is only 6 :30. There 
is time enough. But to their horror they discover that 
the clock is stopped. In reality it is 6 :40. They dash 
to the telephone. The wire is busy. Almost frantic, 
they wait for a connection. The picture cuts to the death 
house. The warden has left the office to witness the 
execution. We see the telephone ringing violently, but 
there is no one there to answer it A policeman is 
sauntering leisurely up and down outside of the office. 
He hears the bell ringing and makes a move to answer 
it, but changes his mind with a shrug of his shoulders, 
indicating that it is not his business to answer the tele- 
phona We go back to the frantic girl on the other 
end of the wire. Then we are given a gruesome glimpse 
into the death chamber where the man has been strapped 
to the chair and the electrician is arranging the levers. 



18 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

Then we go back to the policeman. We know perfectly 
well the man will escape in the end, but the suspense 
is terrible. The policanan finally makes up his mind to 
answer the- telephone. He strolls leisurely toward it. 
When he understands the message, however, he makes 
a wild dash for the death chamber. The door is locked. 
He pounds upon the panels, but inside no one pays any 
attention. We see the electrician as he throws on the 
lever . . . and the door is opened. Even yet the direc- 
tor, or author, is not content. The picture fades out, 
giving the impression that the pardon came too late. 
It is not until the next scene when the lovers are shown 
together that our suspense is relieved and the audience 
can bxeathe freely again. The solution of the play 
really came about the end of the third reel with the 
girl's proof that the insane uncle is guilty. The remain- 
ing two reels are given over to thrills of momentary 
tension which cheapen the picture. 

Mr. Griffith often uses momentary suspense to the 
last extreme. It must be said in extenuation, however, 
that he usually makes it subsidiary to his main suspense. 
In " Hearts of the World " his rescue of the girl and 
man from the clutches of the Grermans was prolonged so 
long it became almost ridiculous. Escape seemed im- 
minent when lo! another band of Germans appeared, 
and the fight started all over again. In " The Great 
Love" the same momentary tension is created when 
Lillian Gish is trying to escape from a burning house. 
She has locked herself in the bathroom with the intention 
of taking poison, but when real death confronts her in 
the form of fire, she decides she wants to live. When she 



THE PLOT 19 

reaches the door the key is missing. It had caught in 
the lace of her negligee when she first locked the door. 
She throws herself in a frenzy against the door, while 
all the time we see the key hanging down her back. The 
flames begin to creep into the room. In her terror she 
wrings her hands and tears the lace in shreds in wild 
excitement. Her hands pass within an inch of the key 
as she does sO; but each time she fails to touch it. The 
audience is about ready to shout at her in their excite- 
ment, when heir fingers finally come in contact with the 
key. 

Momentary suspense is also used in the Houdini pic- 
ture, " Terror Island," but here, since the picture was 
created with the single idea of showing Houdini's ability 
to escape from the most extreme circumstances, rather 
than from any attempt to construct a good dramatic plot, 
since the picture is merely a background for his acro- 
batic skill, it cannot be taken as a representative photo- 
play. 

Costume may sometimes be used to complicate the plot 
with great effect. In Cecil de Mille's "Whispering 
Chorus " clothes are used for purposes of motivation. 
The husband has saved up to buy his wife a dress for 
Christmas. She has been watching it in the window of 
the shop for weeks. As he is about to buy it, a friend 
invites him into " a little game." Foolishly hoping to 
double the money, he goes and loses it all. On Christ- 
mas Eve he sees his wife's simple little preparations, her 
little gift packages ready for each one. The knowledge 
that he has nothing for her, stings him to the quick. 
She bravely pretends not to care about the dress, but he 



20 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

can read disappointment back of her twisted little smile. 
Goaded beyond endurance; he sits at his desk pouring 
over his employer's books. He works for a big banking 
.firm to whom a few dollars must mean so little . . . 
just a slight change in the figures. The employer is 
passing through the office. Someone delays him. He 
pauses and turns to speak to his questioner^ laying his 
arm upon Trimble's desL There is a close-up of a rich 
beaver cuff on his fur coat. Trimble looks at it fasci- 
nated. He takes his pen and runs the handle over the 
fur. It rises again richly, luxuriously. The employer 
finishes speaking and passes on to his private office. 
Trimble takes a knife and carefully starts to work over 
the figures in the ledger. We need no leader to tell his 
decision ; we understand his action. 

In another one of Cecil de Mille's productions, " Don't 
Change Your Husband," clothes were used as a factor 
in the plot, but in a different way. Instead of for 
motivation, they were used for identification. The 
husband, always looking for comfort, hides in a niche 
under the stairs at a dance, unloosens his collar, takes 
out a big black cigar, and abandons himself to pure joy. 
He is disturbed by the voice of his friend who is evi- 
dently standing upon the steps of the stairs making love 
to a girl on the landing above him. The audience can 
see the girl, but the husband cannot. He is chuckling 
with glee that he has caught his friend in the act. The 
lover departs and the husband gathers himself up for 
another attempt at the ball-room. A heavy tassel of 
pearls from the girl's dress has fallen to the ground just 
at his feet. He slips it in his pocket, still laughing at the 



THE PLOT 21 

joke on his love-sick friend^ and intending to tell him he 
means to identify the lady of his choice by means of 
the tassel. He forgets aU about it, however, until he 
is at home with his wife. She has laid her evening dress 
across a chair. A pearl tassel lies upon the cushion. He 
looks at it startled, then pulls the other from his pocket. 
It fits into an empty end on the sash, matching exactly. 
The tassel has told its own story. 

In the stage play " The Jest,^' in which the Barry- 
mores played, a cloak is used as an agent in the plot 
as a means of identification. We have become accus- 
tomed to seeing Gianetto Malespino in a white cloak. 
In the last act, a figure closely muffled in a white cloak 
steals into Neri's house. Neri, lying in wait, stabs the 
figure, thinking it. is Gianetto. It is his brother, Gabri- 
ello. In a Japanese play there is something of the same 
idea. A wife madly in love with her husband is 
pursued by a former lover, who holds a sword of Damo- 
cles over her head. He threatens to kill her husband if 
she will not go away with him. She acquiesces. She 
shows him her husband's cloak, and tells him where 
he will lie that night wrapped in the cloak. The lover 
is to enter and stab the husband. That evening she 
urges her husband to take a trip he has long been plan- 
ning. When he has gone, she takes the cloak and goes 
and lies down in her husband's place. The stage is 
darkened. There is a sound of the cry of a whippoor- 
will. There is no need of further action. The play is 
over. 

In the Triangle production of Earl Der Biggers' story 
the " Gown of Destiny " the gown is made the protagon- 



Vt 



A DRAMATIC MOMENT IN CECIL B. de MILLE'S "DON'T 

CHANGE YOUR HUSBAND " 

The husband h^s pulled the tell-tale tassel from his pocket 
and is laughingly reminiscing over last night's ineident. ;. In 
another moment he will discover that it matches the gown which 
is still lying on the chair. The wife's expression already shows 
apprehension and fear, and is in marked contrast with his all 
too unwitting merriment. 



« THE WHISPERING CHORUS " 

An excellent example of the " vision within the frame." " The 
Whispering Chorus," which represents our impulses for good and 
evil fade in and out upon the walls of the cell as John Trimble 
is torn between the desire to save himself from death or to 
preserve his wife's happiness. The calendar upon which the 
doomed man has marked off the days of his life as they pass 
onward toward the fatal day can be seen upon the wall. 

{See page 118) 




The \Vhis|jeriiig Cho! 



tHen opposite page 



THE PLOT 23 

ist of the story. The characters are subsidiary to the 
dress. It enters into the lives of four sets of people, and 
radically changes their destinies. The gown is the only 
thing that holds the dramatic unity of the story to- 
gether. Otherwise it would break up into four separate 
parts. It was a clever story, and unique. The use of 
the inanimate object as the main element in the plot 
was ingeniously handled. Andre, a little dapper designer 
of women's gowns, is on fire to fight for his native 
France. But because he is little and frail, they laugh 
at him at the French Consulate. The Americans also 
refuse his services. Imagine a man like him a soldier I 
That night he goes home, crushed and sad. He dreams 
of a gown that will startle the world, and gets up and 
sketches it. The next day he shows the sketch to the 
owner of the shop. She is enraptured. They make 
it up, and it is beautiful beyond expectations. A woman 
comes into the shop. She is searching for something 
which will bring back her lost youth and he^ husband's 
love. She takes the gown. It is their anniversary, but 
her husband has forgotten, and planned to dine with a 
young girl with whom he is in love. He is about to 
go out when his wife appears upon the stairs. He is 
amazed at the way she has blossomed out. He comes 
back, stays to dinner, and falls in love with his wife 
all over again. The gown has wrought the miracle. The 
wife, feeling that she will never profane that wonderful 
night by wearing the gown again, sends it across the 
continent to her niece who is trying to uphold a fine 
family name without any funds to aid her. She is in 
love with a young Englishman whom she sometimes 



24 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

meets, but she is poor and shabby, and he scarcely knows 
of her existence. She wears the gown to the country 
club, and he promptly falls in love with her. She re- 
fuses to marry him until he returns to England and per- 
forms the military duty which he is slacking. Urged on 
by the vision of her in the gown, he goes and makes good. 
Andrfi's father is a mayor in a little Belgian town. He 
and his wife are held as hostages. A fanatic shoots a 
German soldier and the mayor is ordered shot in re- 
prisal. That night the young Englishman enters the 
town with his men and takes the town, rescuing Andre's 
father from his impending doom. Andre never knows it, 
but he has done his part in the war through the gown 
of destiny. 

There are, then, innumerable ways of using inani- 
mate objects, especially costimies or clothes, as an agent 
in the plot. Torn clothes tell the story of a struggle. 
They may be used as circumstantial evidence. Prosper- 
ous clothes condemn a suspected thief. Shabby clothing 
may bridge over a lapse of time and tell the events of 
the intervening months or years all too vividly. 

Costume may complicate the plot through the device 
of disguise. What would Eosalind in " As You Like 
It " have been able to accomplish without disguise ? Or 
Viola in " Twelfth Night " ? Or Portia in " The Mer- 
chant of Venice'^? What could Lady Harriet have 
done as "Martha" were it not for disguise? And 
Goldsmith's " She Stoops to Conquer " t And Sheri- 
dan's " School for Scandal " ? — down to " Cheating 
Cheaters'' and "Good Gracious Annabelle," there 
are no end of stage plays which depend upon disguise 



THE PLOT 25 

for their dramatic interest. The same device can be 
used with equal effect in the photoplay. Julian Eltinge 
made use of it in a group of plays which he did for the 
screen impersonating women characters through means 
of disguise. Norma Talmadge did it in "She Loves and 
Lies." Mary Pickford did it in "How Could You 
Jean " ? and in " The Hoodlum " ; Douglas Fairbanks 
did it in " Wild and Wooly " and " Headin' South." 
Charles Eay did it in " The Millionaire Vagrant " ; 
Charlie Chaplin used it in " The Adventurer " and in 
" Shoulder Arms " where he becomes a tree. 

Costume, therefore, is not to be entirely ignored by 
the cinema writer. Costumes sometimes speak louder 
than words. Used skilfully it may become a powerful 
ally in intensifying dramatic situations. 

The solution of the plot should be definite and com- 
plete. It should evolve from the events that have gone 
befora The solution of a play should never be purely 
accidental. If in Ibsen's " Doll's House " after Nora 
has shown Torvald the chasm that lies between them, 
how impossible a marriage between their two natures 
is, it had been discovered that the children had scarlet 
fever and Nora had broken down in her resolution say- 
ing " I cannot leave my babies," the audience would be 
unwilling to accept the solution. It would be an out- 
side force that would in no way solve the problem that 
they had seen gradually increasing until it assumes 
gigantic dimensions when Torvald shows that it is him- 
self and his own honor that he places before Nora whose 
only fear has been that he would insist on sacrificing 
himself for her. The irony of it! When Krogstad 




26 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

through the softening influence of Mrs. Linden decides 
not to use the incriminating evidence against Nora, Tor- 
vald cries out that he is safe, the danger is past. To 
Nora it is only beginning. Her disillusionment with 
her " Doll's House " is complete. What other possible 
solution is there than the one Ibsen offers ? She must 
go away until the " miracle of miracles " can happen — 
which is never — a " marriage of spirits between us two." 

Unfortunately in the photoplay there is too much 
stress laid on the happy ending. The happy ending is 
often inconsistent and glaringly out of place from the 
point of view of the artistic. But there have been many 
instances even in the photoplay where the director sacri- 
ficed the proverbial " and they were happy ever after " 
to the logical and relentless working out of his complica- 
tion. In " The Man Above the Law " which was pro- 
duced by Triangle, and was a signal step toward the 
goal of pure characterization, the hero travels toward 
the sunset with his Indian wife and the child of that 
union, while the girl goes back to her Boston home 
heartbroken, but with her spirit strengthened and en- 
riched. It was the only possible solution if the audience 
was to be convinced of the high moral standards of the 
girl which the story had painstakingly built up through 
four reels of continuity, and which were so inspiring 
that her love and strength had raised this man up from 
the depths of vice to the heights of sanctity. It is emo- 
tionally satisfactory. 

In the Famous Players-Lasky version of "Bella 
Donna," in which Pauline Fredericks played the title 
Tple^ the original logical ending was preserved. And 



THE PLOT 27 

strangely enough it was emotionally satisfactory to the 
audience. They would not have believed that this 
woman could ever become regenerate. Poetic justice de- 
manded that she should suffer. And when his friend 
closes the shutters of Nigel's home against her, and she 
goes alone into the wilderness, the play is at an end and 
we are satisfied. 

In the drama examples of sad endings that are emo- 
tionally satisfactory are innumerable. "Komeo and 
Juliet," "Carmen," "Hamlet," "Macbeth," "The 
Second Mrs. Tanqueray," and " The Profligate " (this 
ending was changed for a London audience, but was 
restored in the printed version). "Hedda Gabler," 
and so on ad infiniturru Of course the unanswer- 
able argument in favor of the unhappy ending is that 
the ultimate aim of art is to reproduce life not 
as we would have it but as it is. And no one can 
deny that the world is full of tragic endings to high 
hopes, the affliction of the good and the punishment 
of the innocent. Yet we can satisfy both the artistic 
and the commercial demands by choosing for the 
theme of our photoplays the happier things in life, 
* for it is equally true that joy exists as well as sorrow. 
Disappointment does not inevitably follow ambition, nor 
does laughter always end in weeping. There is a broad 
choice of subjects outside the realm of tragedy and if 
we will but revert to the mid-Victorian belief in the 
Good, the Beautiful, and the True, we can sell the prod- 
uct of our brains and at the same time, perhaps, make 
the world a better place to live in. 

The solution of many plays has come in the form of 



28 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

a surprise to the audience* The name for such solutions 
is the " dramatic surprise/' To be good, the surprise 
must be a part of the main action of the plaj^. If the 
twist is new and original and cannot be foreseen, yet is 
the logical result of what has gone before, it may be 
said to be organic. But the chief interest of 
the story should not rest upon the surprise. Through 
the medium of critical and advertising reviews, and 
through the expression of opinion among people upon 
the plays they have seen, the ^* surprise '' oiteix becomes 
a matter of common knowledge. If, then, there is no 
other appeal in the play outside of the amazing twist at 
the end, the play has no further excuse for being once 
this has become known. The author, even though he 
may have a very clever turn of events at the end, should 
build his play so that the interest in character delinea- 
tion or theme will remain, even though the surprise is 
one no longer. Plays that end with : it was all a dream ; 
it was a joke ; it was a lie ; it was a mistake ; might be 
grouped under this heading of dramatic surprise. Gen- 
erally speaking this is not the best sort of a solution. It 
is, in fact, a lazy solution. The author gets his char- 
acters so hopelessly involved in circumstances that it is 
beyond his ingenuity to rescue them. So he pretends 
that none of these things happened at all. The con- 
scientious author, having entangled his characters, care- 
fully extricates them. Anyone can multiply difficul- 
ties, but it requires skill to solve them. If the author 
merely waives them by declaring they never really 
happened outside of dreamland we are not satisfied. 
We are no better off than we were at the beginning of 



THE PLOT 29 

the play. The whole thing was for nought The audi- 
ence begins to feel that a trick has been played upon 
them — and audiences resent tricks. They prefer one 
character to play a trick upon another, rather than have 
the author play a trick upon them. They like to be in 
the secret* 

The stage play " Seven Keys to Baldpate " made use 
of the dramatic surprise and the device proved to be 
one of its drawing cards. In this play we see the old 
keepers of a hunting lodge on Baldpate Mountain light- 
ing the fires and preparing for a guest. It is winter 
and Baldpate Mountain is a desolate, eerie place, with 
the winds howling through the chimney and the snow 
flying everywhere with the heavy gusts of wind. Pres- 
ently A young man arrives. He is a writer and has come 
to the quietude of this mountain fastness in order to 
write a book which must be finished in twenty-four 
hours. He has a bet with sonie friends that he can- 
write an entire novel in a day. If he can do it anywhere 
it ought to be here at Baldpate Mountain — ^where the 
solitude is intense. He withdraws to his room which 
is upstairs off a little balcony above the living room. 
He sets to work, but is not long to be left in peace. 
Strange events begin to take place. Queer people begin 
to arrive, each one letting himself in stealthily with a 
key. It seems there are some sort of documents in the 
living room safe that someone is scheming to obtain. 
There are thieves and detectives on the trail. There 
is a beautiful young woman in ermine involved. There 
is a ghostly personage with a lantern. It is as if the 
place were haunted by a dozen ghosts. The man is dis- 



30 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

tracted. He finds the beautiful girl bound and gagged 
and apparently dead in the cellar. Then her corpse dis- 
appears only to be found in another placa He spends 
a terrorizing night. He finally rescues the girl, falls 
in love with her, traps all the others and covers them 
Mdth a revolver, when we suddenly discover that the 
whole thing has been nothing but the story which the 
young man is writing. He calmly finishes it and de- 
parts to collect the bet. The audience has been taken 
by surprise. They are amazed and dumfounded. This 
particular example of dramatic surprise has two points 
in its favor : it solves its problems before the surprise is 
disclosed. The surprise is not a trick to escape the re- 
sults of the complication. And secondly, the surprise is 
an organic part of the play. It is a direct result of the 
premise— the laying down of the wager, which we have 
witnessed in the first act. With a little more astute- 
ness the audience might have guessed it for themselves. 

If there is a dramatic surprise in the play it must be 
made worth while. Otherwise when the information 
which has been withheld for so long is finally given to 
the audience, it falls flat. The spectators feel it was 
not worth while waiting for. They say to thenuselves, 
" Oh, is that all it was ? ", and go away dissatisfied. 
Many critics feel this way about " Lady Windemere^s 
Fan.^' The secret about the mysterious Mrs. Erlynne 
might have been told in the beginning — and then there 
would have been no play. There was no particular rea- 
son for withholding it — except to make a play. 

Dr. Victor Freeburg, author of " The Art of Photo- 
play Making," has devised the following list of aids for 



THE PLOT 81 

needy cinema plojts which may be of benefit to the writer 
whose plots are weak in some spot or another. 

I. If the plot is hackneyed: 

1. Transfer the action to some other setting. 

2. Transfer the action to some other period — ^modernize 

or go back to an earlier time. 

3. Transfer the story to some other social stratmn. 

4. Get a higher or lower point of view. 

5. Turn the theme or pattern of the plot upside down — 

do just the opposite of what you started out to do. 

6. Solve the familiar problems in a new way. 

7. Let the familiar ending be led up to by a new 

problem. 

8. Sometimes two or more hackneyed situations may 

be combined in such a way as to produce novelty. 

n. If the plot lacks acting value: 

1. Focalize on one character. 

2. Give the central character more to do. 

3. Make the personalities more complex. 

4. Make the central character an agent instead of a 

victim, or vice versa. 

HI. If the plot lacks suspense: 

1. Add an antagonist. 

2. Add an obstacle. 

3. Add a problem. 

4. Emphasize one of the questions in the mind of the 

spectator. 

5. Baise the main question earlier in the plot. 

6. Answer the main question later in the plot. 

7. Prepare the audience for the climax by introducing 

some direct or indirect reference to it early in 
the play. 



82 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

IV. If the plot is not convincing: 

1. Supply motives for the action, i.e., provide causes 

for the effect. 

2. Magnify the causes. 

3. Diminish the effects. 

4. Eliminate accident and coincidence. 

6. Instead of ending the plot with "it was a dream/' 
"it was a lie," or "it was a mistake," contrive 
a logical solution. 

6. Make the solution an organic result of the preceding 
complication. 

V. If the plot is not strong enough: 

1. Bring the characters into close relationship with one 

another. 

2. Combine two or more characters into one. 

3. Increase the stakes, the winnings, or the losings. 

4. Compress the time of the action. 

VI. If the plot lacks camera value: 

1. Substitute visible elements for audible effects, si^ch 

as song, music, etc., especially if such effects are 
of cardinal value in the plot. 

2. Substitute shapes, designs, textures, eta, for color 

effects. 

3. Substitute actions for thoughts or words. 

4. Substitute deeds for theories or discussions. 

VII. If the plot lacks physical movement: 

1. Add more settings — introduce locomotion. 

2. Break your present setting into various sections. 

Vm. If the plot lacks pictorial value: 

1. Change the setting. 

2. Change the grouping. 

3. Introduce other characters. i 

4. Provide other costumes. 

6. Introduce other properties. 

6. Introduce other physical action. 



CHAPTEE III 

THE CHAKACTEES 

The history of the motion picture up to the present 
time shows us that emphasis has been laid upon plot 
and plot structure, rather than upon the delineation of 
character. Writers busied themselves with the problems 
of arousing and sustaining suspense in the minds of the 
audience and cared little whether their characters were 
appealing, logical, or consistent. This exaggerated em- 
phasis upon plot is an indication that the art of the 
motion picture is still in its infancy. It may be 
paralleled by the early examples of fiction writing. 
" Pamela ; or Virtue Eewarded," published in 1740 by 
Samuel Eichardson and the first English novel that can 
be strictly so-called, took its name from the main char- 
acter of the book^ but in reality is given over to incident, 
situations, and negative character drawing. " Joseph 
Andrews," by Fielding, and the novels of Tobias Smol- 
lett, with their exaggerated caricatures are little bet- 
ter as f^r as characterization is concerned. Then 
came the school of mystery, romance, and terror, 
of which Mrs. Eadcliffe, Maria Edgeworth, and 
Horace Walpole are the best exponents. These novels 
are rich in plot interest, thrills and mystery, and cor- 
respond to the motion pictures that have been shown 

upon the screen for the last seven or eight years. None 

33 



34 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

of these novels has given ns any one character which 
stands out in startling prominence and becomes immor- 
tal as the characters of the later novelists have — Thack- 
eray's Becky Sharp, or Dickens^ Mr. Pickwick or Uriah 
Heep. Skill of character portrayal in the novel came 
with time and experience. The first novelists had no 
pattern to go by. They had to create one for themselves. 
Their immediate successors found the pattern ready 
made, and not having to exert their wits upon its crea- 
tion, set about improving and embellishing it. The 
pioneers who had, as it were, stumbled upon the form 
of the novel were busy with the main structure. They 
had no time for detail. Later, however, when the form 
and the structure were well established, masters of the 
art of writing gave themselves up to creating and per- 
fecting character types with the wonderful results that 
readers of to-day marvel at and enjoy. 

So it was with the early beginnings of the drama. 
" The Suppliants " of -^schylus contained no char- 
acter development. Yet a later play of the same author, 
" Prometheus Bound," shows strong characterization, 
with the play actually built around the character of the 
protagonist. 

As it was with the drama and the novel, so has it 
been with the photoplay. The first cry was for plot, 
more plot, and always plot. There must be exciting 
races and chases, intrigues and counter intrigues, con- 
spirators in the cellars and on the roof-tops, innumer- 
able, never ending, with the result that subtle and care- 
ful character development was entirely disregarded. 
Motion picture producers were so overcome by the fact 



THE CHARACTERS 36 

that they could produce motion that they produced 
nothing else. The typical " chase '^ picture was the re- 
sult. Gradually motion for motion's sake alone came to 
be left out of the pictures and action took its place. Now 
motion becomes action, of course, when it accomplishes 
a definite purpose or is given a special significance. A 
boy walking down the street has no particular signifi- 
cance. His perambulations would be purely physical 
motion. But if he is going to the telegraph office with 
a telegram that will tell the heroine her lover is dead, 
his movements have a special import, and automatically 
become action. After a while action came to be applied 
to the characters. How would a person of such a type 
act under this particular set of circumstances? If he 
runs away he is a coward. If he stays and takes his 
punishment he is courageous. Therefore, if the author 
shows him taking the next steamer for the South Sea 
Islands the action in itself reveals him as a moral 
weakling. 

The pendulum has now swung in the other direction. 
The tendency in the field of photoplay writing is to 
make character paramount The typical " movie '^ story 
with its regulation plot and its all too well-known situa- 
tions, is passing rapidly away, and soon will be seen no 
more. The more progressive and ambitious motion pic- 
ture firms al^ looking for stories containing noble 
thoughts and sparks of enthusiasm, with real human 
people who have real purposes in life, and with the 
dramatic situations springing from the dash of wills, 
or ideals. More and more companies are producing 
plays in which almost the entire interest centers upon 



36 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

character delineation. The Triangle Company made a 
great effort when Mr. H. O. Davis was scenario editor, 
to subordinate plot to character. They put on "Mr. 
Opp/^ by Alice Hegan Rice, which is nothing more nor 
less than the character study of a man. Another photo- 
play they produced at the same time was called " The 
Man Above the Law/' already cited as an example of 
the unhappy ending that is emotionally satisfactory. 
It was the story of a man who had lived far in an out- 
lying territory beyond civilization and beyond law. He 
was making money by trading cheap whiskey for the 
valuable pelts and skins of the Indians of a neighboring 
tribe. He had been a man of parts and great moral 
stamina, but had degenerated to the lowest dregs of 
humanity in cruelty, avarice, and passion. As a direct 
contrast to him, comes a gentle little girl from Boston 
to open a Bible school among the Indians. The only 
conflict in the play is the conflict between the char- 
acters of these two. He becomes regenerated and falls 
in love with the girl, pleading with her to marry him. 
But he is already married by tribal ceremony to an 
Indian woman and has a child. The girl eventually 
triumphs and returns to her people while he takes the 
Indian woman and her child and seeks fresh fields and 
pastures new. 

In the Triangle production " The Honest Man," the 
main interest in the story is the thief who goes about the 
country making robbery, through means of his marvelous 
sleight of hand ability, his profession. He encounters an 
old farmer who treats him kindly and trusts him with 
his money because, as he says, " he looks like such an 



THE CHARACTERS 37 

honest man.'^ The tramp is restrained from robbing 
the farmer by this implicit confidence. After a few 
other people have remarked that he looks like such an 
honest man^ he begins to think if he looJes honest he must 
he honest and begins to act accordingly. It is the old 
Shakespearian idea that ^^ nothing is but thinking 
makes it so," all over again. 

Clever characterization, then, is a goal which every 
photoplaywright should aim for^ It gives acting value 
to the story^ and acting value is a commercial asset. 
Every photoplay should have a central character around 
which the plot revolves. The character should have 
personality and individuality, that something which 
differentiates each of us from all the rest of our fellow 
men. In the photoplay it is necessary that the per- 
sonality be capable of translation into tehns of the 
screen. The characteristics must be expressed through 
action. A jealous person is more cinematic than a sus- 
picious person, because jealousy usually expresses itself 
in actions, but suspicions often do not exist outside of 
the human mind. In a novel the characters need not 
reveal themselves through action. They may be por- 
trayed indirectly by the author who can analyze them 
through the medium of words. The same thing holds 
true of the drama. The characters may be revealed 
by the dialogue of other characters. But in the photo- 
play we are more restricted to the direct method. Char- 
acters must reveal themselves through action. There- 
fore, though characters in the photoplay may be endowed 
with abstract traits, care must be taken to see that these 
abstract qualities have some physical reaction by which 



38 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

« 

they may be expressed. Cruelty, for instance, can 
readily be shown on the screen ; vacillation, envy, selfish- 
ness, courage, suspicion, jealousy, untruthfulness, all 
have equivalent action, but characters which are very 
complex and subtle, like the characters of Henry James 
which are purely psychological studies, should be left 
to the medium of words. Such characters do not lend 
themselves readily to screen portrayal. A character like 
Dickens^ Mr. Micawber depends almost entirely for its 
comic effect upon words, and could not be captured by 
the camera. 

An author often may delineate character and moti- 
vate the plot at the same time. In the stage play *^ The 
Law of the Land'' the cruelty of the father is shown 
when he locks the door upon the mother and beats the 
child brutally with a horsewhip. The agonized screams 
of the child are terrible to hear. The mother writhes 
in horror flinging herself against the locked door. Later 
the same night the mother and father quarrel. The 
father is incensed, and since he cannot beat the woman 
he threatens to do that which will. hurt her more- — 
beat her child. The horror of the first beating is still 
upon her mind. Snatching a revolver, she stands be- 
fore the door. The man thrusts her aside and goes in 
but she shoots him before he crosses the threshold. 
Here the character of the father and the motivation 
for the murder were accomplished simultaneously in 
one scene. 

Sometimes character may be delineated by means of 
inanimate objects. We have already spoken of dress as 
an aid to plot and as an agent therein. Dress may also 



THE CHARACTERS 39 

proclaim the character of a person more loudly than 
words. The shy, the sensitive, the shrinking girl does 
not array herself in low cut dresses and form fitting 
gowns. In Cecil de Mille's ^*Why Change Your 
Wife" the contrasting characters of the wife and the 
model are shown by the high necks and conservative 
modes of the one and the backless, sleeveless trans- 
parent creations of the other. Later when the solution 
of the picture is reached and the director desires to show 
that the wife's strict ideas have been modified as a con- 
cession necessary to holding her husband, the point 
is carried across to the audience by her change of 
costume. 

Mr. GriflSth often uses costmne to delineate his char- 
acters. In " The Great Love '' the unsophistication 
of little Susie Broadplains is brought out by her out- 
landish clothes. She is so anxious to be beautiful in 
the eyes of her youthful lover. She has heard of ladies 
wearing tulle as an adornment, but she does not know 
how they wear it. She manages to get some and she 
ties it around her head like one might tie a bandage 
for a bad toothache. With her sho^ dresses and her 
hair down her back the effect is perfectly ludicrous, but 
infinitely pathetic. She is so young, so innocent, so 
childishly appealing. 

In " True Heart Susie " outlandish clothes once more 
figure in the characterization, but this time they stand 
for sacrifice. In order to put the boy through college — 
an ambition h^ has cherished since his earliest days — 
Susie sells her cows and chickens and mortgages her 
little farm, allowing the boy to believe the money comes 



40 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

from a stranger in the city who at one time showed a 
spark of interest in him« Poor Susie has nothing left 
to buy clothes with and so must wear the old things that 
were left in her mother's trunk in the attia Presently 
when the boy is finished college, and Susie is poorer 
than ever, on account of her last efforts, a little milliner, 
dressed in the prettiest and daintiest of new-fashioned 
clothes, comes to town. The boy completely loses his 
heart to her. The contrast between queerly dressed, 
old-fashioned Susie, and the little new-fashioned mil- 
liner is a constant reminder of Susie's sacrifice for the 
boy. Yet through that very sacrifice she loses him. 
She has no more money to compete with her rival, and 
though she slaves night after night feverishly trying 
to remodel old clothes, it is hopeless. The boy marries 
the little milliner. Again there is that pathos that pulls 
the heartstrings. She has struggled so long and so 
bravely, only to be met with bitter defeat, defeated by 
her own faithful generosity. 

In " The Romance of Happy Valley '* we have again 
this vain striving to measure up to his standards. He 
is ambitious to go to the city, and she works vainly but 
valiantly with fashion pictures, paper patterns, and 
cambric to make herself so beautiful that he will want 
to stay with her and not seek beauty in far places. But 
on the very day when she achieves her creation — a poor 
ill-fitting gown it is, but marvelous in her eyes — ^he 
leaves Happy Valley. Again there is the pathos of 
failure. The dress comes to symbolize all her fallen 
hopes. It has taken into its fabric the deadly dis- 
^ appointment of the little wearer. There could be no one 



THE CHARACTERS 41 

in the audience so callous as not to feel it, or so obtuse 
as not to understand its sad and subtle little appeal. In 
each case the heart interest of the picture centers upon 
those queer clothes and all they stand for, sacrifice, strug- 
gle, and failure. In " Broken Blossoms " even the bit 
of ribbon and the comb, lone relics of a onetime mother, 
which Lucy keeps hidden away, are symbolic of her 
craving for beauty, of her instinct to reach out for 
something beyond the ugliness and squalor of her life 
with " Battling '^ Burrows. Just as the cheap little 
treasures that were so precious in her eyes were hidden 
away from him, so was the beauty of her childish soul, 
her thoughts and her dreams. This is brought out 
again when the Chinaman takes the wonderful silketi 
robe that he has long cherished and puts it on her. He 
appreciates the beauty in the little drudge, and this is 
symbolized by his attempt to clothe her in fitting rai- 
ment. The silken garment stands for his conception of 
her character. 

Another way in which character can be expressed is 
by the attitude of the other persons in the play toward 
the hero or heroine. In "Alarm Clock Andy," a 
Charles Eay picture, the lackadaisical and timid nature 
of Andy is cleverly brought out in the premise by the 
way in which the other boarders in the cheap boarding- 
house impose upon him. When he is already late for 
work the boarding-house mistress tells him to drop little 
Izzy at the kindergarten on his way. Andy rages while 
she waits to find Izzy's hat and rubbers, but he says 
nothing. Dinner is cleared away when he comes home 
from work, so he goes to bed hungry because he has not 



i 
42 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

suflScient courage to ask for a piece of bread. The old 
maid uses his room during the day for her sewing and 
leaves it untidy with the bed full of pins. She keeps 
him waiting until he has to go out without breakfast 
and is late for work while she turns the only bathroom 
into a miniature laundry and proceeds to wash her en- 
tire wardrobe. When even the proverbial worm fails to 
turn, the audience is convinced of his lack of will power 
and self respect. 

Setting is often used to portray character. In one 
photoplay the heroine is shown leaning over a balustrade 
looking out to sea. She is magnificently dressed, tall 
and haughty in her bearing. On the wide balustrade 
beside her struts a beautiful peacock. The significance 
of the bird cannot be missed. 

In seeking about for characters to portray, authors 
usually start with real life. They borrow traits from 
their relatives^ their friends, the people with whom 
they come in contact in their daily life. Very often they 
start with the characters whom they know best of all — 
themselves. It must be remembered, however, that the 
mere copying of real life is not always the best sort 
of character drawing. People in real life are often 
illogical and inconsistent. They possess traits that are 
in direct opposition to each other. Therefore such 
people cannot be lifted bodily from the world around 
us and put into a story. They would appear unconvinc- 
ing and unreal on the screen. Traits of character may 
be derived from whatever source the author may choose, 
but he must blend them into an interesting and logical 
whole. The integrals must be brought together in 



^ 



THE CHARACTERS 43 

perfect proportion. Sometimes the knowledge of facts 
hampers the imagination of the creator. 

There are many precedents in literature for borrow- 
ing from real life for purposes of fiction. Jane Austen 
took many of the characters for her novels from the 
clerical scenes and environment of her father's parish. 
Uncomplimentary though it may be, Dickens found the 
prototype for Mrs. Jelly hy in his mother, and for 
Mr. Micawber in his father. A recent novel makes the 
young English poet, Rupert Brooke, its hero. When 
this is done care must be taken to make the action of the 
story have a definite bearing upon the characters so 
the characters will not appear to be aloof from the plot. 

Mere physical peculiarities do not make character, 
but they may connote certain attributes. A red head, 
for instance, stands for a violent temper. Shifting 
eyes connote dishonesty, a lantern-jaw suggests deter- 
mination and bull-dog tenacity ; slouching shoulders and 
shuffling feet represent laziness; curly hair, irrespon- 
sibility and frivolity; arched eyebrows, supercilious- 
ness. Yet we have all known people who had one or 
more of these physical qualities who did not possess the 
accompanying moral quality, and on the other hand, we 
have known people who were hot-tempered, lazy, or 
frivolous who bore no outside indication of it in their 
face, gait, or manner. However, the author usually re- 
lies upon the accepted connotations of these physical 
peculiarities as signifying the characteristics he desires 
to delineate. Ordinarily physical peculiarities are com- 
bined with spiritual or intellectual attributes. When 
physical attributes are in direct contradiction to mental 



44 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

or moral qualities we have originality. The big strong 
man who is an abject coward ; the puny-faced weakling 
who proves himself the bravest man in a telling situa- 
tion ; the butterfly woman who makes the supreme sacri- 
fice of self; the bad man who turns out to be noble and 
honorable in the highest degree — all are examples of 
contradictory physical and moral qualities that make for 
originality. In Barrie's " The Admirable Crichton '^ 
the character of Crichton, the butler, an inferior by 
position, proves himself the superior of all the men in 
the household, despite their marked advantages of Uni- 
versity training and social integrity. In the William 
S. Hart plays there are frequent examples of the bad 
man who turns out to be good, as for instance, " Blue 
Blazes Rawden," "Selfish Yates," or "The Poppy 
Girl's Husband." Care must be taken in developing 
these unique combinations that originality does not 
carry the author to too great lengths ^and cause him to 
make his characters ridiculous and impossible. 

We have said that it is characterization which gives 
acting value to a story. And from the commercial point 
of view acting value is essential. It does not matter 
whether the character is good or bad, as long as it con- 
tains possibilities for clever interpretation. Character 
drawing may also add to the plot value of a story by 
arousing or sustaining suspense in the minds of the 
spectators. Will the character improve ? Will the char- 
acter degenerate? Or will the character remain static, 
whatever may be the forces which are brought to bear 
upon it? These are the forms of character suspense 
into which a plot may resolve itself. In Hugo's " Les 



THE CHARACTERS 46 

Miserables " the character of Jean Valjean is an ex- 
ample of both character regeneration and stability of 
character. In the first half of the book we are interested 
in the growth of his spirit toward better things. Having 
attained nobility of character^ he is suddenly put to the 
supreme test. He has successfully buried his past^ he 
is at the height of his career, no one will know of the 
defection. Will he allow an innocent man to suffer for 
his offense of long ago? We are in great suspense as 
to whether he will maintain the high standards he has 
finally achieved. He measures up to our expectations. 
We are not disappointed. In Dickens' " Tale of Two 
Cities " there is an example of character regeneration 
also in the person of Sydney Carton. Character degen- 
eration occurs in " Macbeth." Lily in Sudermann's 
" Song of Songs " is an example of stability of char- 
acter, differing from Jean Valjean, in that she remains 
bad throughout the play, instead of good. She is tested 
and found wanting. Stability of character, therefore, 
does not require that a character be universally good 
throughout. The person may exhibit evil tendencies. 
The point is that the character must remain unchanged 
no matter what situation may arise, or what influence 
may be brought to bear upon it. 

Critics of the drama have laid down the following 
rules for the character of the hero. He must observe: 

1. The doctrinal conventions: 

a. The existence and providence of Ood. 

b. The truth and beauty of religion in general, 
c The immortality of the soul. 

d. The existence and malice of sin and the final triumph 
of justice. 



46 CINEMA CKAFTSMANSHIP 

2. The moral conveiitions : 

a. The hero, if he would hold the sympatlhy of the 

audience, must, at least, during the action of the 
play, observe the ten commandments. 

b. For the heroine, this rule is absolute, admitting only 

of such exceptions as prove its luiversality. 
a The villain may be accredited with all the sins in 

the calendar, 
d. The minor characters may have many faults, but 

must show soma stock of solid virtues. 

3. The ideal conventions: 

a. The drama must give us what real life so rarely does 

— ^the triumphant resolution of a set of difficulties ; 
the anguish of virtue and the triumph of sin must 
be changed to the glorifications of virtue and the 
eternal punishment of its enemy. 

b. The hero and heroine must, in general, possess youths 

beauty, goodness, exalted self sacrifice, and un- 
alterable constancy. 

The photoplaywright may apply these principles to 
his character delineation with profit. They make for 
loftiness in thought and moral tone. It has been charged 
against the photoplay that it distorts our ethics and 
perverts our sense of moral values. The accusation has 
many instances to substantiate it^ but the blam^ rests 
rather with the small minds and twisted morals of the 
people who are responsible for the production of such 
plays, rather than with the photoplay as an art form. 
There have been obscene dramas, but we cannot draw 
the generalization that all drama is of that type. The 
photoplays that will have lasting value, that will live 



THE CHARACTERS 47 

in the minds of the people, are those in which the char- 
acterization is fine and noble. Just as the evil char- 
acters in the world's history die, and the great ones 
live on forever, so with the people of the drama, of 
fiction, of poetry, and of the shadow screen. Lincoln 
lives ; Booth is forgotten. 



CHAPTER IV 
THE SETTING 

In writing a story, whether it be for the magazine, the 
stage, or the screen, there must be present three cardinal 
elements : the plot, the characters, and the setting. They 
are not, however, listed in the order of their importance. 
The setting is by no means last and least. It is co- 
ordinate and co-equal with plot and characters. All 
three must be closely and irrevocably bound into a per- 
fect whole, each as important as the other two and no 
one capable of being subtracted from the story without 
radically weakening the entire fabrication thereby. In 
"A Study of Prose Fiction" Bliss Perry speaks of 
setting in the following terms : " In the ideas of set- 
ting are involved the background, the milieu, the man- 
ners, and the morals of the age, the all-enveloping forces 
of historic movements. Or the background is the cir- 
cumstances or events enveloping the characters and ac- 
tion of the tale. The characters and plot may sometimes 
count for nothing if the atmosphere, the time and place 
— ^the background — is artistically portrayed." 

Such a stressing of the importance of setting some^ 
what confounds the demands of certain producers of 
motion pictures that writers make the settings of their 
photoplays neutral, so that the pictures may be taken 
hither or yon, according to the location of the company 

48 



THE SETTING 49 

when work on production begins. This, of course, is 
to eliminate cost of transportation. It is a commercial 
restriction that is incompatible with art. It should be 
said in defense of producers, however^ that not all com- 
panies insist upon neutral settings. Even years ago, 
when companies were not investing such enormous sums 
in photoplays as they are investing at the present day, 
and when there was not the same meticulous care of 
detail as is exercised nowadays, the Kalem Company 
sent a whole company into the Holy Lands to film the 
scenes necessary for the production " From Manger to 
Cross '^ in which J. Henderson Bland, the English 
actor, was taking the part of Christ. Companies are 
working all over the United States taking pictures in 
the locales best suited to their stories. They send the 
cast to Truckee to take snow scenes, or to The 
Bahamas for picturesque island scenes, or to a lumber 
camp in the far North for realistic pictures of the 
logging industry. Lately the Famous Players-Lasky 
Company has established headquarters in England with 
the idea of sending out companies to make pictures upon
