the continent as occasion may arise. The pictures which 
they plan to produce requiring English settings they 
can picturize upon their native heath. 

In no other form of the story-telling art is the setting 
as important as it is in the photoplay. In the r^alm of 
fiction the setting is merely described. It is painted 
in words at the beginning of each bit of action. and the 
reader must hold it before his mental eye while the 
action is unfolding. In the photoplay, as in the stage, 
the setting is seen simultaneously ^th the action. It is 



mil iiiiwi<ft '- 



60 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

constantly before the gaze of the spectators. It cannot 
be divorced from the plot Therefore, the setting must 
not be distracting. As Eobert Edmund Jones, the 
new apostle of the marvelous in scenic effects, says: 
" Scenery is not there to be seen, it is there not to be 
seen." It must be in harmony with the action and 
intensify . its meaning, wherever possible. We have 
said that this is equally true of the stage setting. But 
here again the photoplay goes one step farther. The 
screen has this advantage over the stage : the setting of 
the stage play must be always artificial no matter 
whether it represents within doors or without. The au- 
thor of the photoplay has all out of doors to choose 
from and a great deal of indoors as well. The settings 
are real. They are natural. Houses are made of brick 
and wood, not of papter-mdche. Flowers are growing 
in the gardens instead of being merely painted on can- 
vas. Moreover the stage play must confine itself to 
some two or three settings as a general rule. Of course 
there have been instances when each act has been sub- 
divided into two or more scenes, as in the case of Tol- 
stoy's " Redemption " in which John Barrymore 
played a season or two ago. In that play one of the 
acts, was made up of a series of quick successive scenes, 
but even in this extreme case there were not more than 
eight settings altogether. In the photoplay we may have 
eighty or several times eighty settings. We may travel 
from Iceland to the Tropics, or from the Arctic Ocean 
to the southern seas. And these journeys do not tire 
us ; we do not even have to leave our seats. Furthermore 
they are intellectually stimulating because they give us 



THE SETTING 61 

an opportunity to know strange peoples and study scenes 
which otherwise may never have come over our mental 
horizons. They bring to people for whom there is no 
hope of viewing them the scenes of lands other than 
their own. * 

Yet the fact that settings are new or interesting or 
beautiful is not sufficient reason for their existence. It 
may be all three of these things, but it must be some- 
thing more. It must be dramatized. The author must 
give to it some particular significance. It is not suffi- 
cient if a setting be merely in harmony with the plot 
It must be an integral part of the play itself. In real 
life pur settings are of the utmost importance. We are 
influenced by our surroundings or we react from them. 
Crimes have been committed because of environment; 
suicides have resulted from location ; destinies have been 
changed because of a mere accident of place ; therefore 
why should not the setting be made a dramatic force in 
photoplays ? Why should it not be of equal importance 
to the characters who move upon the shadow stage? 
There is no reason why it should not be made a dra- 
matic force in the photoplay, and every reason why it 
should. Mr. Thomas Ince, director and producer of 
motion pictures, in speaking to the students of photo- 
play composition at Columbia University said that he 
believed that the day would come when settings would 
be entirely done away with and only a black back-drop 
used instead of natural settings. Should such a day 
ever arrive the director and author will have lost one 
of the most potent factors of the photoplay. Such a 
substitution would mean a reversion to the primitive 



62 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

stages in the evolution of the drama. In the Theatre 
of Dionysius in Athens where the masterpieces of 
Sophocles, -^schylus, and Euripides were first produced 
there were no settings for the action. The low dressing- 
house facing the concentric circle of seats was the only 
stage or background the Greeks knew. In " Prometheus 
Bound " there was in all probability some sort of a make- 
shift for the rock to which he was chained. In " Medea " 
there must have been a contrivance above the dressing- 
house in order that the sorceress might appear in her 
magic chariot and be spirited away. These contrivances 
were of the crudest sort and who can gainsay that 
" Promletheus " or " Medea " might not have been even 
greater in their production had they possessed the ad- 
vantage of our modem stage equipment of lighting and 
scientific devices? Is not a modem presentation of a 
Shakespearian play, with a Henry Irving or an Ellen 
Terry in the cast and the settings as carefully worked 
out as our present day leaders in stagecraft can make 
them, more interesting and even more realistic than the 
plays as Shakespeare and his company produced them 
at the old Globe Theatre in London ? If they were not, 
we should be slavishly trying to resuscitate the " apron 
stage,'' " the pit,'' and the " groundlings," and aU the 
other concomitants of an Elizabethan production. To re- 
turn to these earlier crudities would be to retrogress. It 
would entail an admission that the drama has been 
standing still for all these years. The photoplay, even 
more than the drama, can utilize the effectiveness of 
setting. It borrows from the old miracle plays the 
idea of natural setting. The miracle and morality 



THE SETTING 68 

plays traveled from town to town throughout the shires 
of England giving performances on the village green* 
They used the natural setting for backgrounds just as 
the photoplay does. The photoplay, however, goes fur- 
ther and dramatizes the natural setting, while in the 
mirade plays the natural setting very often did not 
harmonize with the subject matter of the play. Usu- 
ally the theme of those early plays were bits taken 
from the Bible. Now an English green or common 
does not in any sense suggest Palestine or the Holy 
Lands, and the imagination of the spectators had to be 
responsible for the creation out of plain everyday terra 
firma of the swirling waters of the flood with Noah's 
Ark riding in triumph upon it in the days of the deluge. 
In the photoplay we have banished this self-imposed 
deception forever. We do not have to imagine the 
natural setting for the action. We actually see it. The 
story is shown exactly where it took place and as it took 
place. And since the screen has achieved this wonder- 
ful advantage, this illimitable power for dramatic ap- 
peal, why should we relinquish it for the black back- 
drop? Obviously and by nature the photoplay is a 
series of pictures. Without pictures it cannot exist. In 
real life we have our high seas upon which storms arise 
and ships are wrecked and men are drowned in. We 
have houses which go up in flames or shelter us from the 
elements or harbor domestic tragedy. We have woods 
in which to picnic or get lost or be struck by lightning; 
we have country roads for farmers' wagons or auto- 
mobiles or nature loving pedestrians. We can pro- 
duce these scenes in the most minute detail upon the 



,V-. --^'-A- 



64 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

screen, and this reproduction is one of the glories of 
the photoplay. Why should we resort to the artificial, 
for we have yet to see a human being basking before a 
black back-drop as his natural habitat, What the pro- 
gression of the photoplay will eliminate as it grows 
doser and closer to perfection is that terrible abuse 
which Mr. Alan Dwan once facetiously referred to as 
" the forests of Bronx furniture," supposed to suggest 
"grandeur." This conglomeration of all the known 
periods, Jacobean, Sheraton, Queen Anne, Louis XIV, 
and a few that never were known besides, are fallaciously 
chosen to represent the home of a man of cultured tastes 
and artistic tendencies. They create a false impression 
upon the minds of the spectators who are not wise 
enough to discriminate between the false and the true. 
The sooner these expensive sets are done away with the 
better for the photoplay, the better for the spectator, and 
the better for art. 

Settings may be simple and yet be an organic factor 
in the plot. Only when it is impossible to divorce a set- 
ting from the action and characters can it be said to be 
dramatized. The setting actually plays a part in the 
plot when it: 

a. delineates character, 

b. carries along the thread of narration, 

c. acts as an antagonist or obstacle, 

d. expresses a thought through symbolism. 

" The Garden of Allah," by Eobert Hichens, is an 
excellent example of the skilful use of setting. In this 
story the desert plays an important part in bringing 



THE SETTING 86 

the young hero to the momentous decisions which form 
the plot. It is the silence and the solitude of these great 
waste places which drive the young Trappist monk 
nearly mad, until he decides he must break away from 
them and get back to the peopled world. And later 
when he and his wife are come, years afterwards, to 
the desert a terrible sand storm arises and in the still- 
ness of the night and the presence of danger and death 
he is brought to realize anew the power of the Deity and 
the obligations which he has wantonly cast aside. In 
that long night he decides that he must return to take 
up again his broken vows and the life of the monastery. 
Without the desert the story would have no power or 
florce. It is a story of the desert evolved from its 
setting. 

The same effect is obtained in the photoplay " The 
Devil Stone," produced by the Famous Players-Lasky 
Company with Geraldine Farrar as the star. In this 
play, instead of the desert it is the ocean that plays a 
part in the plot. The scene is laid upon the shores of 
Brittany. The girl belongs to the simple fisherfolk of 
the village. Throughout the play the restlessness of 
the sea continually beating upon the shore is symbolic 
of the restless nature of the girl. The sea strikes the 
emotional note of the story, but it does more than this. 
It is not merely a symbolic background. It becomes 
an agent in the plot and casts up the green stone which 
is destined to play such havoc in the life of the heroine. 
And it is only when the sea solves the problem by 
taking the devil stone back unto itself that the sus- 
pense of the play is relaxed. In Mary Pickford's play 



66 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

" The Pride of the Clan," the sea enters into the story 
in the same fashion. The old craft which is no longer 
seaworthy has been lashed to its moorings and used as a 
home for the head of the clan. It is only when the 
ocean, made especially wild by a violent storm, carries 
the leaky old craft out to sea, thereby endangering the 
life of the girl, that the hero finds that life without her 
is useless ^nd that social distinctions drop away in the 
face of love. Thus the setting participates in the plot. 
In the Famous Players-Lasky version of the play 
" Prunella," originally written for the stage by Gran- 
ville Barker and Laurence Housman, the settings are 
unique and effective. The play itself is an allegory; 
it transports the audience into the realm of the imagi- 
nary and the unreal. It does not exist here or there 
or anywhere. Its home is really in the heart of the 
average individual. Therefore it would break the illu- 
sion to place the story definitely if the action took place 
in a typical city or a typical suburban town, or a typical 
village. It would be to transport the spectator from 
the realm of visions to the province of every day. And 
it would confine the lesson of the story to one particular 
locality. Therefore the director, Mr. Maurice Toumeur, 
ingeniously arranged to have aU of the settings artificial. 
This device preserved the fantastic quality of the story 
and allowed ample opportunity to express the allegory 
through symbolism. The quaint little house itself stood 
for the human heart ; the garden wall and gate its bar- 
riers. When Pierrot finds the key and opens the gate 
he enters in as love to the heart of Prunella. There is 
an interesting example here of the way setting may be 




" In a quaint, little houae on Uie edge of the town 
There oiicp lived a maid who was taught to look down. 
Prunella Misa Marguerite Clark." 

,17 .1 . ■. -'"Prunella") 



THE SETTING 67 

used to carry along the thread of narration. The little 
house is quaint and gay and sweet on the night when 
Prunella goes away with Pierrot. Time flies by; the 
lovers drift apart. Prunella, who has become known as 
Pierrette, is broken hearted and wanders away. Pierrot 
is unable to find her, but at last, coming upon a grave 
marked " Here Lies Pierrette,? he realizes at last the 
quality of his love. Thinking he may find some solace 
at the little house where first love came to him, he re- 
turns. But the little house tells all too plainly what has 
happened in the meantime. Its windows are broken; 
its gardens wild and unkempt; and a sign hangs from 
the door " To Let." Lovo has gone out of the little 
house, as it has gone out of their hearts and left the 
empty shell, forlorn and desolate. In the early part 
of the story it had been spring in the garden, with 
flowers and trailing vines and birds nesting everywhere. 
Now winter surrounds it and the garden is bleak and 
dreary. Then too, in the early part of the story Pierrot 
brings a ladder to climb to Prunella's window. It ia 
wreathed with roses and he descends it laughingly with 
Prunella in his arms. Now again he calls for a ladder, 
thinking he hears her calling, but the roses have dropped 
away and only the bare and ugly wood of the ladder 
remains. The room above is empty, she is not 
there. The statue of Love still stands in the 
garden, but his bow is broken, his music is silenced. 
And so the setting tells the story that love is dead. It is 
as plain to us as if it were cried out from the house- 
tops. Later Prunella wanders back again and Pierrot 
finds her asleep in the garden at the foot of the statue 



58 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

of Love, half covered by dead leaves. Straightway the 
garden becomes a fairyland of flowers. The snows 
melt away. The setting tells us that love has blossomed 
once more. 

The setting likewise may be said to carry along the 
thread of narration when it shows a decision made by 
some one of the characters in the play. In a certain 
photoplay a young man is tempted to acquire funds 
fraudulently. The scene fades out upon his inde- 
cision. A subtitle tells us three months pass by. The 
new scene irises in upon the same young man, only the 
cheap boarding-house room has been replaced by a 
luxurious apartment, and his tattered clothes have be- 
come of the finest texture and cut. We do not need to 
be told what his decision has been. The setting in- 
forms us. The same thing has been done with a girl who 
has to choose between two suitors, a rich and a poor 
man, only this time it is the neat and trim little kitchen 
with its rows of shining pans and kettles that tell the 
story of her choice. 

There is an old aphorism that says "a man is 
judged by the company he keeps." He is also judged, 
not merely in photoplays, but in real life, by his sur- 
roundings. Therefore the setting may be used to put the 
audience in possession of the necessary facts concerning 
a nian's character. The untidy, the sporty, the ex- 
travagant, the sordid, the healthful out-of-doors, each 
setting marks a character as its ovm. If we want the 
audience to believe the hero is clean and honest we 
would not think of putting him in a vicious gambling 
den. Kather we would have him galloping over the 




The artifit-ial setting uaed by Mr. Maurice Tuurneur, director, and 
Mr. Ben Carre, scenic artist, in the produotion of " Prunella '' ia illus- 
trated aliove. The tree and moon are painted cardhuard. the grass ia 
of cloth with the flowers sewed on at set intervals. Yet the effei-t is 
an exquisite pirtorial mument in the production. Note how costume 
la used to gain emphasis in the composition by throwing the white in 
high relief against the black background. It is also need to show the 
change of character when love transforms Prunella into Picrotte, 



r 



THE SETTING 69 

hills or climbing trails breathing the strong fresh air. 
The girl who is shown playing beside a pond of lilies, 
feeding a white swan, could never by any possible 
chance suggest a vampire in the minds of the audience. 

The symbolic setting may also be used to carry along 
the narration. In one photoplay, for instance, a man 
is sitting by a library table. He is old and his span of 
years is well nigh run out. There is a candlestick on 
the table beside him in which a candle is burning low. 
The fire on the hearth is dying down. The film flashes 
to a scene of other things' and presently comes back to 
the old man. The fire has fallen to ashes, and even as 
we look the candle flickers and goes out By the power 
of suggestion we know the old man is dead. 

In the already mentioned photoplay " The Source," 
the setting entered into the plot of the play. The scenes 
were laid in a lumber camp in the far north. The log- 
ging industry was carried on by means of a swift flowing 
river that passed through the camp. A rival company, 
seeking to cut off the water supply of the lower camp, 
dammed the river. The law, represented by a few 
crooked individuals, who had been previously " bought 
up," backed the latter. The climax of the play occurs 
when the dam is dynamited and the waters come rush- 
ing down in torrents. Without the river there could 
have been no story. The plot is the logical outcome 
of the setting. In fact the plot has its entire being in 
the setting. 

Shakespeare's " Tempest " is another example of the 
dramatized setting in which the setting takes a definite 
part in the plot. Upton Sinclair has dramatized the 



60 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

stock yards of Chicago in his novel " The Jungle " and 
the Pennsylvania mines in his " King Coal." Any 
student of setting v<rho examines these stories even 
casually cannot fail to see that the plot cannot be di- 
vorced from the setting. There is no " neutral setting " 
here. 

Mrs. Humphrey Ward, in one of her novels set in 
the Canadian Rockies, used the setting as symbols of 
the characters. The Englishman in the story was repre- 
sented by the quiet, mild, charming pastoral scenes of 
English rural life. His character was of the same mild, 
quiet, conservative order. The other man was a product 
of the Canadian Rockies: wild, rugged, full of fresh- 
ness and strength. His character suggested the grandeur 
of the mountains, and his breadth of view savored of 
the vastness of the magnificent country around him. 
The giri is endeavoring to decide as to whether to re- 
turn to England or remain in Canada. But her decision 
is really between the two men — ^the settings and char- 
acters are so closely identified. In the end, she chooses 
the magnificent new country, and the pioneer who is its 
exponent. 

Bret Harte^s stories would be impossible without the 
west. Mary Wilkins Freeman's stories could not exist 
outside of New England. Stephen Crane evolves his 
from the sights and sounds of war and battlefields. The 
list could continue indefinitely. Many of our best 
writers have set the precedent by starting with a setting 
and building their story to fit it. The story is told of 
Robert Louis Stevenson that when first he went to 
Saranac Lake in search of health he was almost over- 



THE SETTING 61 

come with the mystery and romiince of the dark Adi- 
rondack woods. He pondered and pondered what sort of 
a story ought to grow out of such a marvelous setting. 
Finally he said : " Now to my engine " — his signal for 
beginning hard work — and set about writing "The 
Master of Ballantrae." 

Both Scott and Dickens frequently began with a set- 
ting, and so did Poe. The setting of " Kob Roy," of 
" Kenilworth," " The Heart of Midlothian,'^ " Oliver 
Twist," " Old Curiosity Shop," " Great Expectations," 
" The Fall of the House of Usher," have all been most 
skilfully dramatized. 

And if this is true of fiction, if the wielders of the 
pen can turn the setting to such good account, how much 
greater an asset is the setting to the maker of plays 
which are going to be seen in every detail instead of 
read. The cinema composer should realize the possi- 
bilities of the setting, possibilities for beauty, for in- 
tellectual stimulus, for dramatic effect. He should 
study pictorial composition, color values on the screen, 
shadows, tones, architectural effects — everything per- 
taining to the art of making settings. Beauty on the 
screen should not be confined to the Bruce scenics, the 
Chester-Outing pictures, the Paramount-Post Nature 
Pictures. All of the beauty condensed in their eight 
hundred or a thousand feet of film — ^the usual length of 
scenics — is at the disposal of the director of photo- 
dramas. The backgrounds for his action should be as 
beautiful as the scenes in the so-called scenics. Nature 
is a gift to all the world. As Lowell says in his prologue 
to " Sir Launf al " : 



62 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

^^For cap aad bells our lives we pay 

Bubbles we buy with a whole soul's tasking. 

'Tis Heaven alone that is given away 
And only God may be had for the asking. 

No price is set on the lavish summer. 

June may be had by the poorest comer." 

With a little patience, a little discernment, and a 
great deal of cultivation of the artistic sense on the 
part of author and director, our photoplays might con- 
tain scenes as beautiful as any canvas, and yet bearing 
closely upon the plot of the story, enhancing and in- 
tensifying the dramatic suspense. And this with half the 
cost of the present elaborate sets. Houses and furniture 
go out of fashion. Rivers and valleys, mountains and 
sunset skies, never do. A photoplay with this dual ap- 
peal to the eye and to the heart, or to the mind and to the 
emotions, may be precious enough to save for a future 
generation. And so we shall come to have photoplay 
classics, screen dramas that will withstand the tests of 
thought and time. 



CHAPTER V 

ADAPTATION 

The present demand on the part of the producing 
companies for copyright material — a demand which has 
grown out of the advertising value of books which have 
become well known to the public in printed form — ^has 
created an entire field of its own for the attention of 
the photoplaywright or cinema composer. This is pro- 
fessional adaptation. From the artistic standpoint, of 
course, adaptation has no excuse for existence. Photo- 
plays should be composed directly for the screen, 'v\4th 
screen values and screen needs in mind. Material that 
has been composed to be read is often far from fitted 
to cinematic presentation. However, we shall take up 
this discussion in greater detail in a later chapter. 
Suffice it to say that adaptation has entered into the 
film world and has assumed tremendous proportions 
until it has become a profession in itself. Therefore, 
it behooves every student and prospective writer of 
photoplays to master the principles of dramatic adapta- 
tion in order that he may handle his material as skil- 
fully and as ingeniously as possible. 
/ When adaptation does not merely reproduce an effect 
i but emphatically improves upon it and recreates it into 
I something greater and more beautiful, then we may look 
upon adaptation almost as an original art. Shake- 



\ .. 



63 



64 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

' speare's art may be said to consist of just that — a 
genius for adaptation. He borrowed his plots from his 
immediate predecessors, from the " Gesta Eomanorum," 
from Hall and Holinshed's " Chronicles of England, 
Ireland and York," and from numerous other sources, 
j lifting them bodily for his plays. Hamlet is nothing 
\ else but Kyd's " Spanish Tragedy '* resuscitated in a 
j different guise. But such were the marvelous char- 
1 acterizations which he created out of the puppets of the 
\ originals, and such was the strength and virility of the 

1 themes which he worked into the plots, that we regard 
him as an artist of the first rank. 
With all due respect to Shakespeare, however, the 
photoplaywright has a problem of even greater dimen- 
f sions confronting him than had that master of the 
; dramaturgic art — without the genius to accomplish it. 
I Shakespeare worked always with the same medium 
i — ^the medium of words. But the cinema writer must 
; translate or transfer his material frqm one medium to 
j another. He must be able to translate from words into 
I pictures. The writer's art is to assemble words in such a 
way as to express thought. His greater or lesser skill as 
a writer depends upon the power and beauty with which 
he handles his words. He is aided by punctuation, para- 
graphing, spacial arrangements, or if he be a poet, by 
the laws of versification and rhythm, and of stanza divi- 
sion. The photoplaywright, however, works with a 
more complex medium. Pictorial composition, symbol- 
ism, real nature, the mechanism of the camera, as well 
as words, all are at his disposal. Therefore, since the 
two media, that of language and that of pictures, are so 



ADAPTATION 66 

essentially different the adaptator' cannot hope to trans- 
late literally from terms of one into terms of the . 
other. He should aim to produce the same effect or to 
create the same emotional or intellectual impression 
upon the eye of the spectator that the original has pro- I 
duced upon the mind of the reader. 

A narrative that has been written to be read usually 
has to be altered in two ways for the screen. The action 
must be made cinematic, and it must be made dramatic. 
Let us first take up the problem of making the action 
cinematic — that is making it capable of being captured 
by the camera. Darkness, for instance, is not cinematic. 
The only way, oddly enough, that darkness can be shown 
on the screen is by the presence of light. If it is neces- 
sary to^show a totally darkened room, it may be done by 
flashing a torchlight about, or by having a single ray of 
light enter from a lamp-post standing in the street 
directly outside the window. 

Sound is not cinematic but can be suggested in a 
variety of ways. The violinist with his instrument 
suggests music. The slow swinging of a pendulum 
suggests the ticking of a clock. The hammer of a bell 
vibrating against the gong suggests the ringing of the 
beU. A musical score thrown upon the screen suggests 
music When the sound has some important bearing 
upon the plot it is necessary to represent it as strik- 
ingly as possible. In Tolstoy's " Eesurrection " the 
screen version of which Pauline Frederick interpreted, 
the music of some wandering gipsies works upon the 
emotions of the girl in the play so strongly as to moti- 



« THE COPPERHEAD " 

An example of excellent adaptation which faithfully preserved 
the spirit of the original. 

First Location 

Exterior for the Town of Millville, Illinois, built on Long 
Island for the screen production ** The Copperhead/' in which 
Lionel Barrymore was starred. This picture represents the 1846 
period, the time of the Mexican War. 



Second Location 

Millville as ij was remodelled for the 1S61 period. It is an 
example of how the setting may enter into the story by showing 
the passage of time and the changes that have taken place. Note 
the addition of fences, pump and boardwalk. This set was 
changed still further for the 1863 period to show the ravages of 
War in the little town, the fences fallen into decay, the paint 
peeling off, dingy and ill-kept yards, etc. 



Third Location 

Millville remodelled for the 1904 period. Note the significant 
.addition of telegraph poles, wires, asphalt sidewalks and curb- 
stones, post-ofSce and comer store. Care was taken to paint out 
the stars in the flag flying from the courthouse, to correspond to 
the number of states in the Union at that time. 




" The Coppei'heaA." 



ADAPTATION 67 

vate her suWquent action. This was shown very cleverly 
by balancing the action. It is Easter Day and the girl 
is shown flinging open her casement window and won- 
dering what the day wiU bring to her. The caU of the 
road seems to be strong in the gipsy blood within her. 
She has fallen in love with the young Russian officer 
who is spending his holiday in the house. The rest- 
lessness of love is upon her. Down the road we see a 
band of gipsies traveling along playing upon their 
native instruments as they go. We cut back to the 
heroine leaning out of the casement window. The 
gipsies come into the picture opposite her window and 
play. She lilts back and forth with sheer joy at the 
music and taking a coin from her pocket, throws it 
impulsively to the singers. Thus the emotional note is 
struck in the beginning of this day which is to have 
such fatal consequences. Night comes on. It is the 
last few hours of the young officer's visit. The gipsies 
have encamped on the other side of the road opposite 
the house. Their fire burns warm and red, throwing 
out its crimson glare upon the white snow. The heroine 
looks from her VTindow at the scene. The musicians 
are playing again while the gipsy band sway back and 
forth, keeping time to the music. A gipsy girl is 
dancing in the firelight. We see the heroine again. 
Her hand is on her heart a3 she listens. We can almost 
hear the music with her, so startling is the suggestion 
of sound. We can sufficiently appreciate how stirred 
are her emotions to carry along our sympathies in the 
face of the ensuing events. Without this understand- 
ing of the effect of the music upon her hot and im- 



68 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

pulsive heart the sympathy of the audience would have 
been entirely lost. 

Color is another element that has no cinematic value. 
Color can be reproduced of course, but not cheaply. 
The Kinemacolor pictures, and more lately, the Prizma 
Films, have been experimenting with color and attain- 
ing fairly satisfactory results. They have confined 
themselves chiefly to nature subjects with an occasional 
spectacular picture, as the Prizma Picture " Our 
Navy," but no five reel dramas have thus far been pro- 
duced. It is an open question whether or not the re- 
production of color gives any advantage to a photoplay, 
granting that the day may come when color can be 
reproduced far less expensively than at the present day. 
The grays and blacks and whites blending into shadows 
or standing out in clear-cut lines are restful and pleas- 
ing to the eyes, whereas color might grow tiresome and 
monotonous. The reproduction at best is artificial. We 
cannot absolutely capture the colorings of nature. And 
as far as costume is concerned the taste of one person 
often offends the taste of another. An unhappy color 
combination on the part of the person or persons respon- 
sible for the costumes would ruin an entire picture for 
a sensitive audience. It is better to let the imagination i 
have full play, to let each spectator clothe the char- 
acters in the colors that appeal to him, and to exercise 
his individuality without disturbing the same process 
of imagination in the mind of his neighbor in the next 
seat. This stimulus to the imagination, this opportunity 
to allow each person to enter into a region of his own 
creating is one of the joys of the spectator of the photo- ) 



ADAPTATION 69 

play. Therefore it would probably add nothing and 
take away a great deal were the soft tones of present 
photoplays superseded by color. 

In order to make material cinematic in adapting 
it to screen purposes it is necessary that the story con- 
tain physical movement, that is, of course, movement 
that can be given dramatic significance. A story in 
which the struggle takes place entirely within the mind 
of one of the characters does not contain cinematic 
action. " The Tell Tale Heart," by Edgar Allan Poe, 
is an example of inward conflict that cannot be cap- 
tured very well upon the screen. It is the story of a 
man who has committed murder and is finally led by the 
torturings of his conscience to confess the deed. There 
is perhaps a bit of action here and there, but on the 
whole the story takes place entirely within the mind 
of the murderer. The reader of the story will observe 
that no motive was given for the murder. In fact Poe 
carefuUy eliminates any thought of motive. 

" Object there was none. Passion there was none. I 
loved the old man. He had never wronged me. He had 
never given me insult. For his gold I had no desira I 
think it was his eye. Yes! It was this! One of his eyes 
resembled that of a vulture — a pale blue eye with a film 
over it. Whenever it fell upon me my blood ran cold; 
and so by degrees — ^very gradually — ^I made up my mind to 
take the life of the old man and thus rid myself of the 
eye forever.*' 

This paragraph robs the act of the three motives which 
usually actuate crime: revenge, passion, and desire for 
gold. The single reason offered is a unique one and 



70 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

suggests the hallucinations of a madman — ^fear of the 
vulture eye. This is all very well for a short story, or 
sketch, for " The Tell Tale Heart " can scarcely 
be termed a story. But the photodrama is based upon 
the laws of cause and effect and there must be in every 
instance premises for the resultant complications. The 
story goes on to show how the would-be murderer entered 
the chamber of his victim every night, just at midnight, 
for seven nights. But it was only upon the eighth 
night that he found the eye opened and was able to 
accomplish his purpose. The room was ^^ as black as 
pitch, with the thick darkness, for the shutters were 
close fastened through fear of robbers." Here again 
we have elements that are not cinematic. The repeti- 
tion of the same act for seven nights would be monoto- 
nous and tedious upon the screen and the darkened room 
would not "register." 

The next few paragraphs are taken up with ^ de- 
scription of the silence that prevails in the room sur- 
charged with the terror of the old man who feels his 
hour has come. 

^' I knew that he had been lying awake ever since the first 
slight noise, when he had turned in his bed. His fears had 
been ever since growing upon him. He had been trying to 
fancy them causeless, but could not. He had been saying to 
himself * it is nothing but the wind in the chimney^ — ^it is only 
a mouse crossing the floor, or it is merely a cricket which has 
made a single chirp.' Yes, he has been trying to comfort 
himself with these suppositions ; but he has f oimd all in vain. 
All in vain, because death, in approaching him, had stalked 
with his black shadow before him, and enveloped the victim. 
And it was the mournful influence of the unperceived shadow 



ADAPTATION 71 

that caused him to feel — although he neither saw nor heard — 
the presence of my head within the room. 

" When I had waited a long time, very patiently, without 
hearing him lie down, I resolved to open a little — a very, very 
little crevice in the lantern. So I opened it — ^you cannot 
imagine how stealthily, stealthily — until, at length, a single 
dim ray, like the thread of the spider, shot from out the 
crevice and full upon the vulture eye." 

It is clear from the last sentence that all of the 
preceding action had taken place in the dark — ^and 
darkness of itself (as we have said before), does not 
" register '* neither have the old man^s fears any physical 
reaction. They existed only in his own mind and heart 
The murderer quickly commits the deed urged on by 
the beating of the old man's heart, which has increased 
as his terror grew greater. So loud was the noise of the 
heart to the waiting murderer that he was afraid in 
his mad excitement that the sound could be heard by 
neighbors. He pulled the heavy bed over his victim 
until the heart had ceased. He then dismembered the 
corpse — an impossible and revolting procedure for the 
photoplay. Such things can be read without causing 
the reader to faint from horror. They cannot be por- 
trayed in vivid reality upon the screen. He hid the 
corpse under the scantlings and then in perfect con- 
fidence let in three oflBcers of the police who had been 
told that a shriek had been heard by a neighbor during 
the night. 

^'Suspicion of foul play had been aroused; information 
had been lodged at the police office and they (the officers) had 
been deputed to search the premises. 



72 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

"I smiled — for what had I to fear? I bade the gentlemen 
welcomB. The shriek, I said, was my own in a dream. The 
old man, I mentioned, was absent in the country. I took 
my visitors all over the house. I bade them search, search 
well. I led them, at length, to his chamber. I showed them 
his treasures, secure, undisturbed. In the enthusiasm of 
my confidence I brought chairs into the room and desired 
them here to rest from their fatigues, while I myself, in 
the wild audacity of my perfect triumph, placed my own 
seat upon the very spot beneath which reposed the corpse 
of the victim. The officers were satisfied, my manner had 
convinced them. They sat while I answered cheerily. They 
chatted familiar things, but ere long I felt myself getting 
pale and wished them gone. My head ached and I fancied 
a ringing in my ears; but still they sat and still they chatted. 
The ringing became more distinct; — ^it continued and be- 
came more distinct: I talked more freely to get rid of the 
feeling, but it continued and gained definitiveness — ^until at 
length I found that the noise was not within my ears." 

The noise increases and the murderer is convinced that 
it is the beating of the old man's heart as it lies be- 
neath the planks of the floor. He tries to talk above 
it lest the officers hear it, but in the end, tjonvinced that 
they are only making a mockery of his horror by pre- 
tending to disregard it, lie admits the deed, pulls up the 
planks, shrieking, ^^ It is the beating or his hideous 
heart." 

To all intents and purposes this story would be the 
last possible choice for screen material, but oddly 
enough it was adapted to photoplay form and by that 
master artist, Mr. D. W. Griffith. However, in order 
to make sufficient action for a five reel feature, he 
combined the poem "Annabelle Lee" and the atmos- 



ADAPTATION 78 

phere of the Poe tales of horror together with " The 
Tell Tale Heart " and a little of his own imagination 
and produced " The Avenging Conscience." The vulture 
eye, which is uncinematic as it stands, it being impossi- 
ble to get an actor with such an affliction, and it being 
doubtful whether the vulture tendencies would 
" register," could one be obtained, is represented by a 
black patch worn by the old man over his right eye. 
This is disfiguring, and conveys the impression that is 
gotten from the descriptive words of the story. The 
younger man, whose part was played by Henry B. 
Walthall, is made the ward of the older man and is 
youthful and attractive, thereby gaining the sympathy 
of the audience, which the character in the original 
does not hold at any time. The old man is anxious that 
the younger pursue a literary career, and so we 'see the 
young man poring over his books. Excerpts from one 
of the volumes, which happens to be a volume of Poe 
in which his favorite theme of guilty conscience is set 
forth, are thrown upon the screen. This puts the 
audience into the weird atmosphere of Poe^s writings. 
The boy is in love with the girl Annabelle Lee, who 
lives over the fields in a neighboring cottage, but the 
old man disapproves of the friendship, feeling that the 
boy spends time with the girl which would better be 
spent upon his books. It so happens that the boy and 
girl are planning to go to a garden party one afternoon. 
The boy has been delayed by his uncle, and the girl 
waits for him at their trysting place. The time goes 
by, and she becomes a little nervous. Perhaps there 
has been some mistaka She starts to go in search of 



74 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

the boj^ and gets as far as the garden gate, but hesitates 
to approach the house. At last, fearful that something 
has happened to the boy, she goes to the door. Her 
character is shown by her pretty hesitancy and 
timidity. Several times ishe is on the jwint of knock- 
ing but withdraws her hand. Finally she knocks 
timidly, whereupon the door is flung open by the irate 
uncle who denounces her, saying she is nothing but 
a common woman of the streets pursuing his boy. 
Annabelle stands overwhelmed with grief and sur- 
prise at the insult. The uncle turns to find the boy, 
who has overheard the denouncement, blazing with 
wrath and fury. Here then we have a basic motive. 
The old man had struck at the younger one in the most 
vital way — ^through his beloved. The boy insists upon 
following Annabelle and going to the garden party, but 
it is a sad affair. Annabelle feels that their love cannot 
go on if the boy^s guardian looks upon her in such a 
light, so as darkness falls the lovers bid each other 
farewell forever. On the way home the boy stands 
looking at a spider^s web, and watches the spider devour- 
ing the flies caught in his toils. He reflects that 
maturity, like the spider, eats up the happiness of youth 
remorselessly. His heart is hurt and raw with the in- 
justice that has been heaped upon him and his love. 
The feeling grows, and that night when he looks at 
his uncle asleep over his books, he can restrain him- 
self no longer. He snatches up a pillow and suffocates 
the old man. 

And then the avenging conscience begins its work. 
Mr. QriiBSth had to solve the problem of representing 



ADAPTATION 76 

the sound of the imagined beating of the old man's 
heart. He does it in three ways. A friend of the boy's 
father comes to the house, and he alone, with the 
exception of Annabelle, believes the boy guilty. The 
boy has concealed the corpse in a chimney place, much 
the same as did the murderer in " The Tell Tale Heart." 
The man, as he talks to the boy, keeps tapping with 
a pencil in an even rhythm upon the table. The sound 
evidently annoys the boy, who becomes very nervous and 
asks the man to stop. The man stops tapping with 
the pencil, but a close-up of his foot shows once more the 
even rhythmic motion suggesting the beating of the 
heart The boy stands the sound as long as he can, 
and then in a frenzy begs the man to desist He does. 
But from time to time he looks toward the clock upon 
the wall, the pendulum of which is shown swinging 
back and forth, back and forth, in the same rhythmic 
movement The boy can stand it no longer. To him it 
isi the beating of the old man's heart He flings himself 
upon the clock, tearing out the pendulum and confessing 
his deed. 

At this point, the last traces of Poe's story end, and 
Griffith's begins. To make up the needed five reels of 
material, the story develops into a ..typical " movie 
chase " picture. The boy escapes and takes refuge in 
the barn, where he fires upon the sheriff's posse, which 
attempts to capture him. At last, his ammunition 
exhausted, he hangs himself from a rafter of the bam ; 
while Annabelle, heart-broken, plunges from a cliff 
into the sea. As the picture fades down it dissolves 
into the living-room with the guardian sunk down in his 



76 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

armchair, and the boy starting up in terror from sleep 
in the chair opposite him. He is overcome with relief 
to find it has all been a bad dream. He goes over to his 
guardian full of affection and remorse. But the old 
man has not been asleep after all. He has been think- 
ing things over, and has come to the conclusion that 
love is love and youth is youth and must be served. 
Meanwhile the verse 

"The moon never beams without bringing me dreams of 

my beautiful Annabelle Lee, 
And the stars never rise but I see the bright eyes of my 

darling, my Annabelle Lee." 

has been flashed upon the screen, and we see Annabelle, 
convinced that love is more powerful than all things, 
coming across the fields to make peace with her boy. 
All of this was done in the davs when the device " it 
was a dream," " it was a He," and " it was a mistake," 
had not been abused and overdone. It was one of the 
first " dream " pictures, and as such was rather new 
and interesting. Now, however, that form of dramatic 
surprise has been worn threadbare by constant repeti- 
tion. The story, of course, cannot be said to be an 
adaptation in the strictest sense of the word. But in 
as much as it has caught the spirit of the original, and 
preserved the basic theme, the workings of a haunting 
conscience, it has conformed to the main principle of 
dramatic adaptation. It is rather a story based upon 
" The Tell Tale Heart" than a strict adaptation of it, 
for no literal adaptation of " The Tell Tale Heart " is 



b 



ADAPTATION 77 

possible since the story as it stands is not cinematic, 
and must in some way be adapted to screen needs. 
^"^ Another principle that must be kept in mind in 
changing material from the terms ofl one medium into 
terms of another is the need for pantomimic expression. 
The action must be self-explanatory. Pantomime must 
not be confounded with acting. The pantomime is 
in a play whether it has ever been acted or not. Act- 
ing is the interpretation by the actor or actress of the 
pantomime put in by the author of the photoplay. The 
pantomime is often called in studio parlance " the 
business '' of a scene. It is the task of the photoplay- 
wright to conceive bits of business that will carry his 
meaning across to the audience. This need not be done 
in too obvious or crude a manner. The power of 
suggestion may be relied upon to convey many subtle 
meanings. 

So much for making the action cinematic Let us 
turn now to our second problem, making the action 
dramatic Plot value, of course, is the first requisite 
for dramatic action. It is this that the photoplay shares 
in common with the drama, the need for struggle, and 
its resultant suspense which makes for plot value. But 
we have already discussed plot at length in another 
chapter. 

The second requisite for making the action of a story 
dramatic is acting value. Acting value exists in a story 
when there is strong characterization. If the characters 
lend themselves to interesting interpretation, if they 
offer opportunity for the skill and power of an actor or 
actress, there is bound to be acting value, and the play 



\ 



78 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

is certain to appeal. In adapting a novel to the screen, 
then, the writer must be sure to centralize the interest 
. upon certain characters. It is impossible to carry along 
in the limited scope of a five reel photoplay all of the 
characters that appear in a three or four hundred page 
novel. Therefore we must eliminate certain insignifi- 
cant characters and build up the action around the 
more important ones. Usually there are not more than 
two or three principal personages in a photoplay. Very 
often stories with good plots have no acting value be- 
cause the interest is spread equally among all the 
characters instead of being focalized upon any one 
person. Sometimes the chief character does not lend 
itself readily to screen interpretation. In a story like 
Sewell's " Black Beauty " thei-e is no acting value in 
the part of the chief character, because it is a horse. 
The same thing holds true of Jack London's " Call of 
the Wild," only here the hero of the tale is a dog. 

The stressing or emphasizing of a character in an 
adaptation may sometimes entirely change the type of 
the story. In the recent novel, " Java Head,'^ by 
Joseph Hergesheimer, appearing originally in the 
Saturday Evening Post, there are two women characters. 
One is a Chinese woman of high degree whom Gerrit 
Ammidon marries and brings home with him to Salem. 
The other is the character of Nettie, with whom Gerrit 
had been in love earlier in the story. Both women are 
interesting character studies. It was said that Mme. 
Nazimova was considering the purchase of the play. 
Had she decided to play the part of Taon Luen, the 
play would have become a tragedy, because that lady, 



ADAPTATION 



79 



r 

realizing Gerrit and Nettie are in love, takes opium and 
dies. But if she placed the artistic stress upon the 
character of Nettie, the photoplay would have come 
under the heading of comedy with the inevitable happy 
ending. 

Dr. Victor O. Freeburg, in lecturing to the students 
of Columbia University upon adaptation, drew up the 
following chart for use in making the action of adapted 
material dramatic: 

r Omit — Incidents and characters which are 
not cinematic and cannot be made dra- 
matic. Example: Mr. Micawber is not 
a cinematic character because he is dis- 
tinguished by his dialogue chiefly. ^ 



Change of Content 

or 

Subject Matter 



Change of Form , 



Substitute — Incidents, motives and settings, '^ 
but retain the m^in situations at least in . ' 
effect. Present main characters with 
their original personalities, development 
and destinies. 

Alter — ^when possible, rather than omit. 

Add — ^Anything that makes the story more 
clear or vivid to the audience. For ex- 
ample: transitional and antecedent action 
must often b^ added for clarity. Multiply 
the settings. 

r Make order of events 
chronological. 
Make happenings and fol- 
lowing happenings si- 
multaneous, i.e., paral- 
lel the plot. 
Make simultaneous hap- 
penings successive. 






.y 



\ 






s- 



Change the order . 



Shift the Emphasis 
(artistic stress) 



r To other characters. 

To other actions. 

To other settings. Pro- 
ducers like to Ameri- 
canize foreign settings 
in order to reduce the 
cost bf production. 



80 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

The list above includes all of the changes that may 
be made in adapting material to the screen. It is not 
recommended, however, that all of them be applied to 
any one manuscript The discretion and discrimina- 
tion of the writer must decide just what changes it is 
absolutely necessary to make in a manuscript. Ideally, 
a story or play ought to be altered as little as possible. 

If a novel or play is altered beyond recognition, it 
can scarcely l)e called an adaptation. Not long ago 
the Vitagraph Company adapted the " Tale of Two 
Cities " to the screen. But since war stories were not 
wanted and the restriction would apply to stories of 
The French Revolution as well as the World War, they 
decided to transport the story from London and Paris 
to the Blue Grass region of Kentucky. Instead of a 
conflict between the aristocrats and the people, the con- 
flict is a Kentucky mountain feud. The only relics of 
the original seem to be the characterization of Sydney 
Carton, and his sacrifice for the girl he loves. He saves 
her lover at the cost of his own lifa The character of 
Sydney Carton is summed up in the title " The Man 
Who Might Have JSeen." Such a deviation from the 
original is to be condemned. In this category also was 
the adaptation of " Jane Eyre," known as " Woman and 
Wife." The setting was changed from an English 
manorial background of the early Victorian period to 
a modem New York apartment. The comment of the 
critics upon this adaptation was that they were glad 
that the title was changed so that the memory of a good 
book was not ruined by the reality of a poor photoplay, 
because, since had it not been called " Jane Eyre " no 



ADAPTATION 81 

one could possibly recognize it as suck. The adaptation 
could not preserve the spirit of the original, discarding 
as it did Thornfield Hall and the niysterious green baize 
door which led to the unfrequented portion of that 
rambling old house — a fitting setting for any dark 
tragedy. " Rebecca of Sunnybrook Farm," in which 
Mary Pickford played, was a good adaptation, the 
evidence of its excellence lying in the fact that even its 
original author was pleased with it, " The Copper- 
head " was likewise admirably done. 

As far as the photoplay market is concerned, adapta- 
tions of books upon which the copyrights have expired, 
are rarely, if ever, saleable. Such books are the property 
of everybody, and producers do not feel they ought to 
buy material which is already at their disposal. They 
have a staff of writers who can adapt non-copyright 
material, they believe, much better than can any hap- 
hazard individual. If an adaptation of an uncopy- 
righted book is made and sent to a company, it may give 
them the idea of screening that particular story, but no 
price is paid for the su^estion. They would in all 
probability return the script, and have a staff writer 
make a new adaptation. On the whole, however, not a 
great deal of non-copyright materials is used. There is 
a further reason why companies dislike to produce 
material of this sort. Suppose they are at work on 
a splendid production of one of the classics, and indulge 
in an expensive advertising campaign. A lesser com- 
pany may hear of this and produce an inferior version 
of the same play or story. The cheaper photoplay 
would profit by the advertising, as well as the company

ADAPTATION 88 

that had incurred the expense. The first company would 
have absolutely no protection. An instance of this 
occurred recently in the filming of the Stevenson story 
"The Strange Case of Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde." 
Two versions appeared at the same time, one a notable 
photoplay, one of the most artistic screen productions of 
the season, employing the talents of a great actor, and 
profiting by the scenario and art experts of a good 
company. The other was a mediocre affair that might 
ordinarily have passed by unnoticed "were it not that 
the public were waiting anxiously to see the " Dr. 
Jekyll and Mr. Hyde" production. Great was the 
resultant confusion, due to the fact that people in 
general usually carry away the name of the production 
itself, rather than the name of the company that is 
producing it. 

Titles cannot be copyrighted, a fact of which many 
unreliable companies take advantage. Several years 
ago a play was announced outside of some of the Brook- 
lyn theatres as " The Inside of the Cup." Many people 
thinking it must surely be an adaptation of Winston 
Churchill's novel of that name, went to see the play. 
It had not the remotest connection with the novel which, 
it transpired, had just be6n produced by another com- 
pany. Of course, it may have been pure coincidence, 
but, " The Inside of the Cup " is an odd title, and its 
reduplication would be almost a supernatural phe- 
nomenon. 

It may be in order to avoid this chance of deliberate 
reduplication of titles that the companies so frequently 
change the names of the copyright novels, short stories, 



84 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

and plays that they buy and produce. Otherwise it is 
inexplicable. Why should a company pay thousands of 
dollars for a famous stage play in order to profit by the 
advertising value of its name, and then retitle it so that 
no one can or will associate it in any way with the 
original? George Weston's story, '' Salt of the Earth " 
was bought by the Famous Players-Lasky Company and 
appeared under the title " Eyes of the SouL'' Now, 
of course the Original was by far the better title, and 
flince it appeared in the Saturday Evening Post, had 
good advertising value. The company realized both 
of these facts and had no intention of chan^ng the 
name until they discovered that there had been a novel 
published under that name which another company had 
purchased and were at work producing. Hence it was 
absolutely necessary to search about for a different title. 
In instances of this sort the companies are entirely 
wise in re-titling their photoplays. Unfortunately, how- 
ever, changes are often made without any rhyme or 
reason. Take " The Bird's Christmas Carol,'' by Kate 
Douglas Wi^n, for example. The name has become 
almost a household word throughout the United States. 
The book was bought by one company for more thou- 
sands of dollars than is usual on account of the popu- 
larity of the author and the book. When the photoplay 
was finally released it was under the title " A Bit of 
Heaven." No one had ever heard of, or was the least 
interested in " A Bit of Heaven," vnth the result that 
the picture was a dismal failure. The reason given 
for the change was that " The Bird's Christmas Carol " 
was too seasonal People would go to see it during the 



ADAPTATION 86 

Christmas holidays, perhaps, but not on Washington's 
Birthday or the Fourth of July. 

If the adaptation has been so altered and combined 
with original material that it is practically a new story, 
an author may sell it as his own product. Not long 
ago the courts sustained the claim of an author upon 
this point. Companies have been known to buy such 
adaptations at the market price of original material. 

As for copyright books and plays, it is useless for the 
photoplaywright to make adaptations of those because he 
does not own them, and therefore is powerless to dispose 
of them. His only interest in them is either as an agent, 
in which case he would get probably a ten per cent 
commission, provided he had the permission of the 
publisher or author to negotiate a sale, or as a profes- 
sional continuity writer. In this capacity he can com- 
mand at least one hundred and fifty dollars a reel. 
But before the production editor will entrust the making 
of a continuity to an individual, he or she must prove 
that he is a trained and skilful scenarioist. 

It would be useless to schedule the prices that a 
copyright novel or story may bring. The sum depends 
entirely upon the popularity of the book, the popularity 
of the author, and the money at the command of the 
purchaser. 

A person who has a good knowledge of continuity 
may get an opening in professional work by asking a 
producing company to allow him to make a continuity 
of some work they intend to produce. If he makes 
one as good or better than their regular writers can, 
he may be offered a position on the staff, or be placed 



< 



86 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

upon the list of those to whom they " farm out " as it 
were, the continuity they want done from time to time. 
These people work in their own homes, coming in to 
the oflSces of the company from time to time for consul- 
tation and instruction. Eve TJnsell, Frances Marion, 
Charles Maigne, Clara Beranger, John Emerson and 
Anita Loos, Edith Kennedy, Marion Fairfax, Ouida 
Bergere, and countless other scenarioists have composed 
under this system of free lance continuity writers. 

Below are the ideas of one of these professional 
continuity writers, Miss Eve Unsell, now on the Lasky 
staff in London, expressed in a paper called " The 
Routine of Film Adaptation," read to the students of 
photoplay composition at Columbia University. It is 
interesting as first-hand evidence upon adaptation given 
by one who has spent so much of her time at the task. 

THE "ROUTINE'' OF FILM ADAPTATION. 

(Read to the Cinema Composers' Club by Eve Unsell, 

Jan. 24, 1919) 

The five-reel feature is here used as an example, since 
this is the average number of reels required for the 
"program" feature. Exhibitors and producers are con- 
vinced that the public wants a short five-reel feature, which 
means on the average, 4,500 feet of film, and some 500 feet 
of titling, and which consists in the script of about 200 
scenes. Exhibitors say that the five-reel feature is the most 
popular with screen audiences, on account of the length of 
the program, which is usually made up of a "weekly," or 
news bulletin, an educational film, and a short comedy, 
the "feature" topping the bill. The exhibitor claims that 
when the feature is longer than five reels the audience 



ADAPTATION 87 

usually becomes restless unless the story is unusually big 
and absorbing, and that, on the other hand, if the feature 
were shorter than £ve reels, the audience would feel that 
it had not had its money's worth, and that the program 
would be too short. 

On account of this set rule for the feature's length, the 
art of writer and director is naturally hampered, for the 
light story must be ''padded," and the big story cut down 
to the required length. It is obviously much easier to tell 
a feature story of any bigness in a picture that is over five 
reels, and it often requires wizardry to keep the footage 
down to the required length. Many a script-writer and 
director have spent sleepless nights in cudgelling their sorely 
tried brains to find a way to preserve the excellencies of a 
story that really demands a six-reel development, but must 
be confined to the 4,500-foot measurement. Often the 
Procrustean methods used in the cutting rooms after the 
film is made result so disastrously to the picture that neither 
director nor writer recognize the finished product — so com- 
pletely "finished" it is I 

The fine Italian hand of the cutting department is also 
one of the reasons for the wails of anguish and dismay 
from the authors of books and plays who see the mangled 
remains upon the screen of a masterpiece in which the 
original intent and purpose of the author had been some- 
thing entirely different to that which the cutter and editor 
of the "revised version" have made the be-all and end-all 
of the picture. 

Unfortimately, the continuity writer usually receives the 
total blame for these revisions, from the public, and at the 
unmerciful hands of the critics of trade-journals and dailies. 
One would imagine that, after years of writing reviews of 
the motion picture, a critic would have more than a speaking 
acquaintance with studio methods, and know that other de- 
partments are " implicated " in the crime, besides the script- 
writer, who is often hardly an " accessory to the fact," 



as CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

4ereti« bdving been in blisfful ignorance of the dumges 
until the excoriation of the critic pillories liim to the iniblic 
yi4g^ ail dseir sole author. You who intend writing: for 
the so'eesi most prepare to receive some thorns among the 
rom^, m begin at onoe to bury any atom of sensitiveness 
you may poetess, for the sling and arrows of an outrageous 
fortune are nothing to the stings and arrows of an out- 
TBgfii critic when he finds his favorite book or play changed 
ti^ond recognition. His rage is only excelled by that of 
the original author, who can scarcely be blamed, since 
his knowledge of studio routine is even less than the 
critic^s. 

At present The Authors' League Bulletin is loud in its 
diatribes against the thievish, slavish, knavish Pariahs of 
the literary world — ^those utterly brainless incumbrances of 
the ground, the continuity writers I 

The only reason that the continuity writer is able to drag 
hirn^lf about after reading such pitiless exposures of him- 
self and his utter unwortbiness, and to continue to' breathe 
ttie pure air he contaminates, is the fact, known to many 
Mcenario departments of the largest studios, that there have 
bd^en occasions when the great Masters of The Authors' 
hmtfxw have themselves come a cropper when essaying the 
*' simple'' task of putting their ideas into continuity form 
for the screen. I have in mind one very clever (dramatist, 
who has had more than one Broadway success to his credit, 
who some months ago insisted — after the sale of a "big 
i(l<?a" to a certain film company — that he, and he alone, 
be allowed to scenarioize the story, having seen too many 
nnitilations of the children of his brain. 

The President of the Company assented, and the humble 
effort of the continuity director was shelved, while the 
Master withdrew to his country home to let ^* genius bum." 
Oenius burned very slowly, and with starts and spurts, and 
it was necessary to jog the gentleman at last and remind him 
gently that a certain very expensive star was eating her 



ADAPTATION 89 

head off in salary at the studio, while the force waited for 
the masterpiece. 

At last, the scenario began to come in, in installments, 
and when the third part had been sent in, and about six 
reels had been consumed without the hero having met the 
heroine, it was decided to call, by stealth, the despised con- 
tinuity writer, who was bidden to begin to link together the 
installments as they came in. One night, after eight o'clock, 
the scenario writer received the last of the lot, and all night 
slaved to make the great author's big idea producible, not, 
however, touching the plot, or the author's development, 
merely arranging and linking together the scenes, account- 
ing for time-lapses, et cetera. Next morning, the director 
was at last i)ersuaded to try to produce what he termed the 
most fearful and wonderful melange he had ever met in 
the course of a good many ye^s of good and bad pro- 
ductions. The studio went ahead, and lived up ^to the 
author's desire to see his picture as "wrote," and in the 
last reel, the heroine and hero at last met, clasped hands, 
and clinched — almost in a breath — and the picture was 
scanned— and canned — ^by the critics as viciously as was ever 
any maudlin effort of any mere, obscure continuity writer. 

Later, the celebrated author's original idea, which was 
really worth while, was artistically developed as a stage 
drama, but it was a far cry from the original scenario of its 
author, and the humble continuity writer was highly gratified 
to note in witnessing the successful stage production, that 
some of the ideas scorned in the scenario-writer's produc- 
tion, which, you will remember, had been previously shelved 
at the author's request, had been incorporated in the play. 
They were probably perfectly obvious ideas, which might 
have occurred to anyone not enjoying the close perspective 
of the author, himself, who could not bear to see one jot 
or tittle of his original idea changed in the scenario. But 
where the lowly scenarioist had been unable to sway him, the 
great manager, who had collaborated with the dramatist.

In th<s process of the idea's transference from the brain 
of \in creator to the shimmering square that conveys its 
mcMMige to the breathless, waiting world is not uninteresting 
to the layman, from the numerous inquiries that come to

A picture/' 

The first thing to determine hi the routine of feature 



ADAPTATION 91 

adaptation is whether or not the feature plot is there when 
the script-writer considers the story that supplies the basis 
of the picture-to-be. When he has satisfied himself as to 
that small item, he sketches out a skeleton form, or synopsis, 
of the drama's action, from which his five-reel feature is to 
be elaborated. Experience teaches him that often in the 
most popular stage plays and "best sellers," there are com- 
plications and situations that are of no screen value, though 
clever enough in the original version, where drags in action 
are not so noticeable as in the picture. And when certain 
portions are condensed, other parts must be expanded, the 
star's part built up, new situations added to " liven " up the 
picture, and motives strengthened, for weaknesses of plot, 
glossed over in the book and play by wise or witty dialogue, 
stand out in ghastly fashion when the bony frame-work is 
brought out unclothed by flesh, in startling vividness by the 
X-ray of the camera. 

Therefore, the writer finds that he must make the action 
sufficiently clear and striking in itself to "carry" the film 
over without its explanatory and decorative dialogue. In 
original stories, naturally the scenario writer is far less 
hampered than when adapting a "famous" play or book. 
With his own material he has his own sweet way, often finish- 
ing with a different and far stronger story than that with 
which he started. But the writer of adaptations must have 
infinite patience, a marvellous sense of the fitness of things, 
and a fiendish ingenuity in blending his ideas and those of 
the original author in altering the material to suit the 
exigencies of the screen, so that his sleight-of-hand work is 
not offensive to said sensitive creator of the subject-matter 
in hand. The path of no scenario-writer is a bed of roses, 
but from personal experience I should say that the adaptor 
has the thorniest way to tread, the hardest, and often the 
most thankless task of all the rest. 

When the "scenario-artist" has combined the old and 
new into a synopsis that seems satisfactory to his firm and 



92 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

himBelf, be proceeds to make a ^' rough scenario" in wliich 
he workn out his screen story, scene by scene, and punch 
by punch. A one line diagram of the action and punch of a 
feature would be a gradually ascending line, starting from 
a small bump, which signifies a mild ** punch *' of action or 
mystery sufficing to command interest in the story at once, 
and from here the up-sloping line leads to a slightly larger 
hillock in the middle, where the dramatic situation or com- 
plication lends a fresh interest and suspense forming a 
stronger " punch'* than the first, and then the line climbs 
on to the mountain punch that forms the climax, the 
scream of the comedy, the solution of the mystery, or the 
denouement of a domestic tragedy in which i)oetic justice is 
dispensed, all around. In his first rough diagram of the 
scenario's action, the writer suggests where subtitles and 
spoken titles shall be necessary, and approximately the 
ideas, though roughly expressed, which are to be stamped 
in them. 

The rough scenario is then submitted to the supervisor 
in charge of the scenario department, from whom it goes 
to the director, and sometimes to the producer, at last coming 
back to the script writer with additions, eliminations and 
various comments which it has been sure to gather in its 
expedition. With the round'-robin of "O.K.'s" the writer 
proceeds to make his final scenario, working out the detail 
of his scenes now, and boiling down his superfluous titling, 
which he polishes carefully, hoping and praying that they 
may be left alone by cutter and editor. There are a few 
writers who do not have to advise with the producer until 
the scenario is completed, but there are also few writers 
whose completed product sees the screen without change. 
There may have at some time been written the perfect 
scenario in which no detail was altered before it was re- 
leased as a picture, but if so, that script deserves a place 
in the Museum of C7n-Natural History! And you who now 
complacently view a picture, and murmur triumphantly to 



ADAPTATION 93 

yourselves, " Goodness — ^how much better I could have 
done it, myself!" — are often quite right, but wait — 
WAITl — ^till you view one of your own — after it has passed 
through cutting room, and the re-editing procedure. After 
that, you may sympathize with many a scenario writer whose 
pictures you have seen — and scorned! 

That is why so few scenario writers can qualify to ad- 
vertise the Seven Sutherland Sisters, for the hair that does 
not moult during the incubation of the scenario is frequently 
torn out by the frenzied scribbler when he sees what has 
happened to his scenario after that magician, the expert 
cutter, has finished with the !film. Forgive me for dwelling 
on this point, but it is a sore subject with most script 
writers, and some day it may be for you, also. 

About the method of titling the film, that differs with 
every company, and in any case, varies often with the 
character of the story to be told. Despite the learned 
treatises on the subject, one soon learns that the rule of 
art is art, old theories are upset every day, and nothing is 
to be gained by the following of any set rule in titling, 
save that usually the conventional explanatory title, that 
makes the action to follow an anti-climax, is to be avoided 
as Satan, himself. And the spoken title must not be awk- 
ward, verbose or imgrammatical — ^not to mention, too fre- 
quent! Yet it is absurd to say that the fewer the titles, 
the better the picture, for too many of the screen's biggest 
plays disprove this theory. Some of the most successful 
features of a certain prominent film company depend greatly 
on the generous sprinkling of clever titles, which point the 
humor, clarify the situation, intensify the psychology and 
emphasize the drama. 

A perfect gem of a picture may be developed with no 
subtitles at all, so well does the material of the story lend 
itself to action only. On the other hand, a picture of a 
different type, but no less great, may demand any ntmiber 
of clever titles to bring out its psychology, and should it be 



i 



M CmEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

treated as ihe firrt phty, the method would prore disastrous 
to photoplaj number two! The result of much ezpeiimen- 
tatioD, sifted down, shows that some fine plays need littie 
titling, while some^ equaUj fine, dranand more titles. 

Insist that, whenev'er possible, you be allowed to work with 
the director or title-supervisor upon the titles, evea if it 
does take more time from your schedule of other work. The 
director is usually anxious to work with the adaptor on this 
matter, but sometimes both are busy on the next production, 
and again the cutter and reviser woik their sweet will upon 
the picture* Veiy xecently I was horrified to see, at a 
leading New York picture house, an adaptation of mine 
bring gales of laughter in the serious and delicate scenes, 
marring the artistry of the beautiful star, and fine direction 
of the able director. It was not the acting, not the story, 
not the directing that brought the irresistible sweep of 
laughter, but the absurd titles which had nothing to do with 
the situations, and plainly jarred with the actions of the 
actors during the scenes in which they were inserted. I 
can imagine the feelings of the actors when tiiey saw the 
production, and the words that had been put in their 
mouths after the completion of the picture. Needless to 
say, the picture was a burlesque on its original self on ac- 
count of the unintentionally humorous titles. When this 
was brought to the attention of the producers, who stand for 
high ideals and artistic excellence in detail, they were 
horrified* and hastily ordered corrections, but alas, the critics 
had already seen it, and the story and writer suffered ac- 
cordingly. Yet even Hamlet, with not an iota of the 
plot altered, with the greatest actors in the world, and 
a Belasco to direct it, could be turned into burlesque 
simply by the dialogue, itself. I mention this, not only from 
the unquenchable impulse to justify myself in this instance, 
but to show you the vast importance of the title to the film. 
As to the value of schools of photoplay technique, it is 
unquestionably advisable to gain a fundamental knowledge 



ADAPTATION 96 

of studio lequirements and script and screen detail in a 
legitimate school of photoplay construction such as yours, but 
once in the game, don't be too much a slave to set rules, get 
your big subject, give it as logical a development and as 
novel a treatment as you can — ^then trust to providence — 
and your Directorl 

To offset the illustration of the loss of hirsute adornment 
when things go wrong, you must know that in no other 
profession, art — or industry — ^whatever you choose to term it — 
are results of your labors so quickly seen, or emoluments so 
promptly paid! And in contrast to the^ gloom! cast by a 
bungled picture, is the glow of happiness that blots out the 
shadowy mistakes of the past — the glor-ee-ous feeling that one 
has not lived in vain — ^that comes from the big successful 
story, made bigger by a fine director, a feature that is the 
product of a harmonious co-operation and mutual imder- 
standing between script writer and director, each working, 
as should all the film departments — not for personal ag- 
grandizement, but for the Perfect Picture! 



CHAPTER VI 
SCENAKIO TECHNIQUE 

Iw the making of a motion picture there is usually 
a wide hiatus between the original author of the story 
and the director who is to produce it in photoplay form. 
It 18 a rare exception when the creator of the story is 
also the director of the film. The photoplaywright chooses 
his material from the world around him, or from the 
storehouse of his brain, and sets to work arranging it in 
the fashion that best suits his purposes. He then sends 
it out to a producing company and in the course of 
events it is turned over to a director who puts it into 
pictures. Early in the history of photoplay production, 
therefore, it was realized that this gap between the 
author and the director must in some way be bridged 
over. The scenario was finally devised to fulfill this 
function of telling the director as clearly as possible 
exactly what the author wanted done. It was found 
that clearness could best be obtained by dividing the 
story into a sequence of scenes. Hence the origin of the 
scenario. 

The term scenario is a comprehensive term which 
covers the following divisions : 

A cast of characters 

A synopsis of the story 

A scene plot 

The plot of action, or the continuity. 

06 



SCENARIO TECHNIQUE 97 

The» term scenario is often erroneously used as 
a synonym for continuity, whereas, although it in- 
cludes continuity, it also includes all the things 
listed above. The scenario is the completed manu- 
script, while the continuity is merely the action of the 
story. 

In writing the continuity, the author starts out by 
dividing his material into scenes. The scene division is 
purely mechanical. It is determined by the amount of 
action that can be taken without moving the position of 
the camera. The setting is the place in which the action 
is staged. A change of setting always constitutes a new 
scene, but a new scene does not necessarily require a 
change of setting. If the camera must be moved so 
that its angle includes a bit of the room or landscape 
at closer range, a new scene results, even though the 
setting remains unchanged. For instance, the scene 
may be laid in a sumptuous Parisian restaurant. Merry 
groups are seated at gay little tables ; waiters are seen 
hurrying back and forth carrying trays laden with food 
and drink. At one table partially screened by ferns 
and potted plants is a young couple. The audience can- 
not readily identify them in the big general scene, so 
the camera is moved a little closer to the young couple 
in order to give the spectators a better view. This, of 
course, since it necessitates moving the camera, would 
be a new scene. Yet the setting is still the same — ^the 
restaurant interior. It may be that the girl is telling 
the man something which requires a close observation on 
the part of the audience of her facial expression — ^fear, 
or love, or some other emotion, which it is necessary that 



98 CINEMA CRAFTSMANSHIP 

the audience should see in order to follow the story. So 
we must have a close-up of the heroine. In order to 
take this dose-up^ the camera has to be moved still 
nearer to the subject. The close-up of the girl forms a 
new scene. 

Beginners often find difficulty in,deciding just where 
the new scene begins. They would have no trouble 
at all if they would only remember that the camera is
